A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

Climate Context:
The geographical location and low elevation of the coastal zone of Bangladesh make it susceptible to sea level rise and associated flooding, with a devastating effect on the lives and livelihoods in the region. The most recent IPCC report (AR4) argues that global sea level will elevate 0.44–0.78 m by 2100 under the high emission scenario (SSP5-8.5). The Bangladesh Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan B.1 confirms these projections for the entire coastline, but with an even higher maximum value of 0.91 m to the west of the country (by 2300) under SSP2-4.5 as in the low GHS emission scenario (SSP2-4.5) sea level rise is projected to be 0.8 m by 2030 and 1.9 m by 2050. The coastal zone of Bangladesh, including Barisal, Cox's Bazar, Chittagong, Khulna, Noakhali, Satkhira, and the southern parts of Dhaka, Jessore, Narayanganj, Pabna, Rajshahi, Rangpur, Sylhet, and Sunamganj districts are particularly vulnerable to sea level rise, salinity intrusion in rivers and estuaries, and storm surges because of their low elevation, ranging from 12 meters above average sea level.

Hence, the vulnerability of coastal people is characterized in three ways: i.e., (1) poor human settlement in low-lying areas; (2) climate-sensitive livelihoods; and (3) scarcity of safe drinking water. The majority of the coastal population lives below 5 m above mean sea level. Most of these communities are small, with most community-built houses their buildings in low-lying areas that are subject to coastal flooding. Most of the houses are built on embankments or raised platforms to protect them from flooding and other natural disasters such as storm surges, high tides.

These people have to spend a significant amount of their earnings repairing


---
**Page 7**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE | 3

A

houses exist. Besides, the coastal communities primarily depend on seasonal subsistence agriculture and agricultural wage labor, which are highly climate-sensitive. Safe drinking water is highly vulnerable to sea level rise and salinity in many parts of Bangladesh. The communities and local-level organizations have a lack of understanding of the present and future climate change impacts and their ability to promote climate-resilient development in the coastal zone.

To respond to these challenges, the primary goal of the project is to develop climate-adaptive coastal community livelihoods through the construction of climate-resilient housing and livelihood technologies. The project will also enhance the capacity of communities and organizations to address climate change impacts in their localities.

The specific objectives are:
1. To construct climate-resilient homesteads for vulnerable communities in the southwest coastal zone of Bangladesh;
2. To develop climate-adaptive livelihoods for vulnerable coastal communities; and
3. To enhance the capacity of vulnerable communities and institutions so that they are able to plan and implement adaptation interventions.

The project will address two key climate change impacts: (i) reduced vulnerability of the coastal community to the adverse impacts of climate change, and (ii) enhanced capacity of the coastal vulnerable community to address climate change issues. The expected outcomes/results are: (i) decreased risk of loss of assets such as houses, livestock, and crops; (ii) increased income from climate-resilient livelihoods; and (iii) improved climate planning and implementation by communities and local-level institutions.

Aligning with the stated specific objectives, the project will construct climate-resilient houses for the most vulnerable households, climate-adaptive farming technologies (e.g., crab farming along with mangrove tree planting), and integrated coastal community livelihoods. The concept of a climate-resilient homestead is built around three main components: (i) a climate-resilient house; (ii) an integrated homestead structure; household-based vegetable cultivation, a summer latrine, a rainwater harvesting system and water storage tank, and a livestock shelter; and (iii) a community-based livelihoods model. This integrated climate-resilient homestead development will protect them from coastal inundation and cyclones as well as help increase households' income. These homesteads will also provide some safe space for women to engage in income-generating activities.

The project follows the GCF agriculture and food security sector guidelines while designing climate-resilient livelihoods. The proposed livelihood interventions build on the first paradigm shift pathway, i.e., promoting household-based vegetable cultivation, a summer latrine, rainwater harvesting system and water storage tank culturally accepted high-value crops and farm animals while providing the required skills and technology upscaling. The project will promote an integrated fruit-fish-fiber model in the southwest coastal areas of Bangladesh.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### 1. Bangladesh's coastal zone is divided into 19 districts along a coastline of 710 km. The coastal zone extends over 47,150 sq. km of area and has a population of 38.52 million (BBS, 2011). The coastal zone is quite distinct from the rest of the country. The coastal zone has been delineated based on three characteristics: (i) high exposure to sea level rise; (ii) vulnerability due to salinity intrusion and cyclone surge (high water); and risks of cyclones and storm surges*. The zone is characterized by a vast network of rivers and channels, which are prone to flooding. The coastal zone also has several unique geographical features such as No Ground (underwater canyon located 45 km south of the Sundarbans in Bangladesh), the shallow northern coast, and the deep southern coast. The average elevation in the southwest coastal zone ranges from 1-2 m and in the southeast coastal zone it is less than 0.5 m. These geographical features coupled with changes in precipitation and temperature play a significant role in its vulnerability to sea level change, salinity intrusion and cyclone and storm surge.

#### Figure 1: Topography of Bangladesh Coast

The proposed project area

2. The proposed project has selected seven exposed districts of Bangladesh's coastal zone. These are Satkhira, Khulna, Pirojpur, Patuakhali, Bagerhat, Barisal and Bhola. These districts have been selected based on their high exposure, vulnerability and the feasibility of interrupting a crab hatchery supply chain in the regions. The following figure shows the proposed project districts.

## Proposed project area

The total area of these seven districts is 24,691 sq.km and total population is 12,275,996. The average poverty rate is 26.13per cent which is higher than the national average of 20.3per cent. The dependency ratio is also high in these districts which is 68.59 (detailed in table 1). Moreover, 52.01per cent of households in these districts are vulnerable to floods and cyclones. # Sea level rise and associated salinity intrusion in Bangladesh coastal zone

The IPCC sixth assessment report stated with high confidence that Asian coastal countries are likely to increasingly experience relative sea-level rise, which will result in the coastal flooding of many low-lying islands and coastal areas. This is consistent with the findings from a large number of scientific studies. Increasing sea level rise is one of the most critical climate change issues for coastal states such as Bangladesh.

The IPCC sixth assessment report stated that the average rate of relative sea-level rise over the past 30 years has been about 2 mm/year, which is higher than the global mean rate (1.7 mm/year). The rate of increase in relative sea level varies across different regions and countries. For example, the rate of relative sea-level rise for Bangladesh coastal areas is approximately 4-5 mm/year.

The IPCC sixth assessment report stated that the average rate of relative sea-level rise over the past 30 years has been about 2 mm/year, which is higher than the global mean rate (1.7 mm/year). ## Tropical Cyclone and Storm Surges

The coastal areas of Bangladesh are frequently hit by cyclones that form in the Bay of Bengal. Over the last 50 years, 15 severe cyclones with wind speeds ranging from 140 to 225 km/h have hit the coastlines of Bangladesh.

### Frequency of Cyclone Occurrence

The highest frequency of intense tropical cyclones has been reported to occur between the months of October and November. The Bay of Bengal is one of the most active regions for tropical cyclone formation, and many of these cyclones have been affected by cyclones in Bangladesh. Cyclone-induced storm surges are more devastating than those caused by high tides.

### Impact of Cyclones

In 2007, the top cyclone that struck the south-west coast of Bangladesh is Sagar. It was a category-4 cyclone that struck the south-west coast of Bangladesh (e.g., Satkhira, Khulna, Bagerhat, Barguna). Pabna district at 28th June 2007. The death toll from Cyclone Sagar in Bangladesh is estimated to be around 350 deaths by the degree of effects by Cyclone Sagar, about one million were seriously affected it? It caused a financial loss of Tk 14 billion and an economic loss of Tk 68 billion. The number of people injured was 279, the number of deaths caused by Sagar is estimated at 3,456, with 1,001 still missing, and over 55,000 people were displaced in the affected areas. The cyclone also caused severe damage to infrastructure such as roads, bridges, and buildings.

### Affected Districts

The most severely affected districts are Satkhira and Khulna followed by Bagerhat, Pirojpur, Barisal, Patuakhali, Bhola, Laxmipur, and Noakhali. The cyclone caused severe damage to infrastructure such as roads, bridges, and buildings.

### Human Impact

The cyclone affected 10 districts of Bangladesh. A total of 279 people were injured in the cyclone, while 350 people from 115,757 families are affected in Khulna, where death toll stands on 45%. Loss of livestock and crops was also reported.

## 10.
The Sixth Assessment Report of the IPCC stated that the number of intense tropical cyclones is assumed to increase (High Confidence). However, it is narrated with medium confidence that total number of cyclones through the world might decline or remain constant (IPCC, 2021). In AR6, the IPCC states that there is high confidence in an increase in the frequency and intensity of tropical cyclones. In addition, it is asserted with high confidence that anthropogenic climate change results in increases in heavy precipitation events and extreme sea level rise, which will likely lead to increases in the frequency of all-category tropical cyclones due to the unavailability of authentic data (IPCC). However, it is noted that there are trends in the observed frequency of all-category tropical cyclones due to insufficient data.

## 11.
In perspective of changes in tropical cyclone patterns, the unambiguous sea level rise (see paragraph at 5.2) will likely lead to more frequent and higher intensity coastal flooding events and storm impacts. Using the IPCC5 scenario, Ummenhofer et al. (2009) argues that there will be a significant increase in the frequency of tropical cyclones with an increase in sea level rise. Similarly, Emanuel (2005) also projects increased intensity of tropical storms by 2100 for the North Indian Ocean region due to the projected increase in sea surface temperature and wind shear. Combined with expected rises in sea-level, these higher intensity cyclones are predicted to cause increases in storm surges in Bangladesh has already experienced about 40 per cent of the total impact of storm surges in the world.

## Coastal Flooding
### 12.
Under natural conditions, areas of the coastal zone are often flooded at high tides, either throughout the year or during spring tides only or in the monsoon season when monsoons entering the region from inland rise to high levels (IPCC). Coastal flooding by saltwater was found on the extreme south-west through the year and especially west and south-east coasts during the dry seasons. Cyclones and storm surges have a direct impact on increasing coastal flooding. An estimated 3.45 million people were

13. This model, in a bid to determine potential future inundation zones by 2050 under the climate change scenario, projects that Bangladesh will be exposed to an increase of more than 40 cm rise in sea level, a 10 per cent increase in wind speed, and the landfall of cyclones during high tide. It is predicted that by 2050 an additional 15 per cent of the coastal area of Bangladesh will be inundated with water due to increased storm surges and higher tides. Not only will the areas of Khulna, Bagerhat and Satkhira Districts be newly exposed, but also parts of the southern districts of Chittagong and Cox's Bazar.

14. Coastal flooding due to tidal surge, along with the huge amount of sediment that is carried by the three major rivers through a slow flow gradient, coupled with the increase in sea level, results in severe flooding in coastal areas. The Bangladesh Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (BCCSAP) stressed the need to understand, prepare for and respond to these emerging challenges so that the wellbeing of the people is ensured (MUEF, 2009).

Impacts of Climate Change
15. Coastal communities are particularly vulnerable to the aforementioned climatic dangers because of their poverty and other challenges, which are explained below.
Vulnerability of homesteads to climate hazards

**Page 16**

16\. Households in the coastal zone are subject to coastal flooding, with more than two-thirds of the households in coastal communities made of mud and goat path that are easily and severely damaged during cyclones. The damage caused by these storms results in loss of livelihoods, income earnings and time on an annual basis for house repairs or reconstruction due to climate related damage.

17\. The construction of communal cyclone shelters, among others, is the main technique used today to protect coastal communities from cyclonic storm surges. However, the effectiveness of such response infrastructures have a number of drawbacks. Due to the lack of adequate sanitation and hygiene facilities in these shelters, many people are reluctant to use them. In addition, many people often reluctant to use such facilities. Many families opt to seek safety in their own homes rather than using these shelters, which leaves them extremely vulnerable. The multipurpose use of cyclone shelters as emergency shelters may also be limited in the near future as a result of the rapid demographic changes in Bangladesh.

Livelihoods and Nutrition

18\. Livelihoods mostly depend on the climate-vulnerable agriculture sector through seasonal subsistence agriculture and farm labour. This is expected to be further aggravated by degrading agro-ecological landscapes, increasing salinity intrusion, and coastal flooding which are likely to be exacerbated by storms.

The gradual increasing proportion of salinity intrusion into the coastal areas (soil, river, and groundwater) of Bangladesh threaten the primary production system, coastal biodiversity, and human health. According to the Soil Resources Development Institute (SRDI), the area under salt-affected land in Bangladesh increased from 10 million hectares in 1973 to more than 56 million hectares in 2000, to 105–106 million ha in 2009, and is still continuing to increase. Salinity intrusion directly affects the livelihoods of farmers living along the coastlines. The area under salt-affected land has been increasing at an average rate decreasing over the last few years due to various factors and studies have identified salinity as the main cause for this trend. The major causes of salinity intrusion in coastal Bangladesh are: (a) since 1970, include: (a) salinity has decreased the production of wheat by 4.42 million ton per year on coastal Bangladesh; (b) since 1985, the area under paddy cultivation has been decreasing and is likely to become rare in coastal areas; and (c) between 1976 and 2006 the number of cultivated winter vegetables has decreased by more than half.

20\. Livestock, including small ruminants, are primarily affected by coastal inundation associated with total surges, monsoon precipitation, and cyclones. As stated earlier, livestock deaths are evident. Moreover, increasing salinity intrusion into the coastal areas is also a major threat to livestock production and food inflation. In the last few decades, the area of grazing land and the intensity of grazing have decreased due to the increase in salinity intrusion. The decrease in grazing land has led to an increase in the raising of large ruminants by causing a scarcity of fodder and animal feed, particularly in the south-western coastal areas where livestock production is more common than in other parts of the country. Besides, SLI-induced salinity, coastal floods, cyclonic storm surges, and waterlogging will cause damage to livestock shelters and sometimes deaths. Despite these challenges, the marginal farmers are still dependent on livestock for their livelihoods as it provides them with a source of rural livelihood and nutrition security. The slatted houses for goat or sheep will protect them from flooding, cyclones, and other natural disasters.

21\. Productive land is being lost due to saltwater intrusion, causing a loss of agricultural yield and fodder, while cyclones and storm surges are responsible for many livestock deaths. Ultimately, this is leading to

22. Increased coastal salinity has affected fish yields, leading to substantial reductions in the inland open-water and estuarine fisheries of Bangladesh. The areas most severely affected are the southern coast (e.g., Khulna (36,500 ha), and Bagerhat (49,550) ha). Apart from these, the production of native fish species such as chitala, chitalakshana, kheringa, kat, singal, tia, khalianna, pokta, and kamangir is gradually declining due to increased salinity.

23. A study in the highly saline Paglaibhanga sub-district in Khulna and moderately saline Rampal sub-district of Bhola district found that fish yields declined from 1975-80 (46 kg/ha) to 1995-2000 (3.5 kg/ha), respectively, during 1975–2005. The slight increase in salt-tolerant species did not compensate for the loss in yield of native fish species.

24. Increases in salinity by 250 and other stresses are predicted to significantly negatively impact the Sundarbans mangrove ecosystem which, in turn, will impact the livelihoods of poor communities who depend on it for their food security, income generation, and coastal protection from cyclones and storm surges and honey production. Salinity has resulted in the loss of 12 percent of marine fish and 25 percent of shrimp species in the Sundarbans. The salinity increase has also led to a decline in the availability of other aquatic products such as fish, honey, and gilapia at local markets. Due to restrictions from the government as part of its conservation efforts, there is no access for poor communities to collect these resources or use their services of Sundarbans. For example, to ensure that wildlife during the mating season had an undisturbed environment following the trend of 2011, the government prohibited admission into the forest and fishing in the area from March to May every year which has led to a decline in fish and honey collection. The demand for locally produced livelihoods is therefore more crucial than the need for livelihoods that depend on fragile natural resources.

Drinking and Sanitation Water

25. Coastal surface and ground water, from which the majority of the population rely for water, is increasingly becoming saline due to increased salinity caused by sea level rise, cycles and tidal surges. Coastal people, particularly women, spend otherwise productive time collecting drinking water from long distances away.

The result of community engagement reveals that local people are aware of water scarcity issues, and nearly all of them perceive that salinity is the main reason behind them. Even though socioeconomic factors such as poverty and lack of access to clean water also contribute significantly towards this problem, such as household-based temporary rainwater harvesting, and the informal use of freshwater wells, these are not sufficient for meeting their drinking water needs in the dry season when there is no access to the freshwater shortage. Inhabitants of those salinity-affected areas spent a significant portion of their income on buying freshwater.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 14

## World Bank Project for national housing market: PKSF has been implementing the World Bank funded project on Housing Finance in Bangladesh since 2015, which is being implemented through 76 local government municipalities and city corporations since October 2015. As of September 2022, PKSF, through its service provider LICHIP, has disbursed Tk 384 million to over 9,000 borrowers for the construction of new houses and the extension or repair of existing ones in 13 towns under 'Shelter Lending & Support' component of this project. This project focused on urban and semi-urban areas where there is a high demand for housing but where access to finance is limited. The project also targeted communities and where communities have less capacity to afford housing loans. The beneficiaries under the LICHIP are those who earn between Tk 10,000 and Tk 25,000 per month. The LICHIP has developed a guideline for implementing housing loan activities for medium- to low-income groups in urban areas. This project is now being scaled up by PKSF with the support of the World Bank under climate-resilient homes. PKSF's experience in building or repairing houses will also be useful for implementation of this project.

## Climate Resilient Infrastructure Mainstreaming (CRIM): This is the first GCF-financed project in Bangladesh. KfW is the accredited entity, and the Local Government Engineering Department (LGED) of the Ministry of Public Works is the implementing agency. The project will be implemented through 12 districts in the country and aims to mainstream climate resilience into infrastructure development by establishing a Climate Resilient Rural Infrastructure and Pilot Climate Resilient Urban Infrastructure. The project will enhance the capacity of LGED to mainstream climate resilience into infrastructure development at national level, by establishing a Climate Resilient Local Infrastructure Centre (CRLIC) in the organization under its umbrella. The CRLIC will provide technical assistance for the design and construction of cyclone shelters with innovative design in climate change aspects and rehabilitate 20 existing shelters to a climate-proof standard in the three project districts (Bhola, Barguna and Satkhira). Under infrastructure mainstreaming activities, the project will also focus on improving water supply and transport infrastructure, with priority given to the most vulnerable, such as the inhabitants of the city slums. The project will be implemented through LGED in 12 districts across Bangladesh. However, in three districts, there is no overlap in project activities. Rather, these two projects will complement each other. In addition to the above mainstreaming activities, the project will also focus on climate resilience of individuals. To avoid duplication of resources, the RHL will additionally conduct formal and informal consultations with the CRIM on the selection of the project sites and beneficiaries. The project will result in a significant increase in the capacity of LGED for mainstreaming climate resilience into infrastructure development.

## Enhancing adaptive capacities of coastal communities, especially women, to cope with climate change induced salinity: The GCF has financed UNDP Bangladesh to enhance the capacity of coastal communities to adapt to climate change-induced salinity. The project is being implemented in 12 upazilas and is estimated at Tk 33 million, of which GCF grant is Tk 25 million and the Ministry of Women and Children Affairs contributes Tk 8 million. The project will provide assistance to 30,000 women and girls in Satkhira and Khulna districts through a community-based approach for adaptation to climate change-induced salinity by strengthening their adaptive capacity at household level. This will be achieved through community-managed rainwater harvesting solutions. It will also seek to strengthen the participation of women and girls in decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation, with continued monitoring and adaptation of livelihoods to evolving climate risks. The UNDP project implements a gender-sensitive approach for the selection of beneficiaries and ensures that women are included as active participants throughout the project cycle. Whereas the RHL will intervene only in the on-farm sector, specifically crab farming and goat or sheep rearing. In addition, the RHL will develop climate-resilient homesteads for vulnerable people living in low-lying areas.

# Extended Community Climate Change Projects Flood (ECCCP-Flood): The GCF-financed project that is being implemented in Bangladesh

It is a four-year project with a total budget of USD 13.68 million, of which GCF grant contribution is USD 9.32 million. The project covers the marine chair areas of Jamnapur, Kalabanga, Kurnia, Lalmoinipur and Bhola. It aims to improve the livelihoods of vulnerable communities in coastal areas through water and sanitation, promoting flood-adapted crop production, and goat or sheep rearing in raised homesteads.

## Goals
- Goat and sheep rearing in raised homesteads as a climate-resilient adaptation strategy for ECCCP-Flood and RHL projects.
- The project will be implemented in an area of 102.5 km² with a population of about 38,749 people living on small landholdings and in a small space, and the operation cost is also very low. It is also critical for household nutrition. In addition, women are involved as they are responsible for livestock rearing.
- The project will be implemented in parallel with their main duties. The project will add improved technology and management systems. The project will be implemented by PAKSF (People's Action for Sustainable Food Security), a climate-resilient NGO.

## Project Impact
- The raised homesteads will protect goats and sheep from coastal flooding, storm surge and cyclone.
- The project is aligned with the Bangladesh Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (BCCSAP) and the Community Climate Change Adaptation Programme (CCCCP), which was completed in 2018. The experience of PAKSF found that goats and sheep are highly resilient to climate change, especially when they are raised on raised homesteads. This is a very good option for survival.
- On the other hand, their productivity is very high. Women can easily get involved. The response of the community to the technology in terms of adaptive capacity and economic benefit is significant.

## Government Support
- The government has established the Bangladesh Climate Change Trust (BCCCT) and allocated USD 10 million for ECCCP-Flood project.
- The project will be implemented by PAKSF, a climate-resilient NGO. The project will also build protective works, building cyclone-resistant shelters, excavation or re-creation of canals, construction of water harvesting structures, installation of solar panels, and introduction of stress-tolerant crop varieties and seeds.

## Additional Projects
- The project has allocated USD 40.6 million for the Coastal Zone Environmental Infrastructure Project (CZEIP), USD 175.8 million for the Climate Change Resilient Infrastructure Project, Coastal Towns Environmental Infrastructure Project, Climate-resilient Infrastructure Project, Coastal Towns Environmental Infrastructure Project, and Income Generating Activities and Climate Change Capacity Building and Knowledge Management project.
- The project will also implement other projects related to this project, such as raising livestock in individual households with raised homesteads.

## Government Support
- The government has also established and implemented the Bangladesh Climate Change Resilience Fund (BCCRF). Funds under BCCRF are used to improve the resilience of smallholder livelihoods through agricultural interventions, i.e., promotion of stress-tolerant crop varieties, cyclone sheltering, water harvesting structures, installation of solar panels, etc.
- The government has been implementing Pilot Program for Climate Resilience (PPCR): Strategic Programs for Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation (SPCCAM), Coastal Zone Environmental Infrastructure Project, Climate-resilient Infrastructure Project, Coastal Towns Environmental Infrastructure Project, Climate-resilient Infrastructure Project, Coastal Towns Environmental Infrastructure Project, Income Generating Activities and Climate Change Capacity Building and Knowledge Management project.
- The project will also implement other projects related to this project, such as raising livestock in individual households with raised homesteads, construction or reconstruction of embankments, safe drinking water, and afforestation, etc.

35\. The Global Environment Facility (GEF) is supporting Building climate resilient livelihoods in vulnerable landscapes in Bangladesh (BCRL) to improve the resilience of people, communities, and ecosystems through adaptation and, where possible, through investments that enhance food security and food systems. The BCRL is a landscape-based action research project. The BCRL project will develop climate-resilient high-value crop advisories in collaboration with Department of Agricultural Extension (DAE), Bangladesh Rice Research Institute (BRRI) and the Ministry of Agriculture. The advisories will be developed, tested, and disseminated using the same system adopted by AMCDP. It will also help improve the overall quality of advisory services provided to farmers through regular feedback from farmers on the usefulness, timeliness, and relevance of information provided (actual feedback loop). The BCRL project is led by BRRI with technical support from the Project Director and Project Implementation Coordinator. The project has been implemented in three landscapes: Khulna, Sylhet, and Chittagong Hill Tracts. The landscape-based approach ensures that the RKL and the BHL are coherent, i.e., two districts such as Khulna and Sunthia districts are common between both landscapes.

The results of the BCRL project in these three landscapes have been promising. In addition to the RKL, the BCRL project focused on fisheries and livestock sectors. In addition, the RHL project will introduce climate-resilient hostels for the poor and vulnerable community. In the future, the projects like BHL and RHL will be scaled up through the Bangladesh Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (BCCSAP) with the Ministry of Finance to the GCF. The proposed project has already been presented to the TAC and it has received positive feedback from the TAC members. The TAC has recommended that the GEF coordinate with the Department of Environment (DoE) which is the Executing Entity (EE) of the BCRL project, for further implementation.

36\. The Adaptation Fund (AF) has approved Adaptation Initiative for Climate Vulnerable Offshore Small Islands and Riverine Bangladesh in 2019. The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), as the AF implementing agency, is executing this project with the Department of Environment, Forests and Climate Change (MoEFCF) is the executing entity. The AF grant amount is USD 3 million. The project will be implemented through a landscape-based approach in the upazila within the Bholia district where is the AF’s project and RHL’s potential activity, i.e., homestead development. The MoEFCC has already held meetings with representatives of the AF project, DoE and UNDP in the site selection process and promote knowledge and learning sharing for the AF project for higher efficiency and efficacy.

Conditional funding from GCF for climate change adaptation

37\. The proposed RHL is in the Bangladesh country programme for GCF under the title “Increase resilience to climate change in southwest coastal zone of Bangladesh through adaptive livelihoods, housing, and soft infrastructure development” (BCRL).

Bangladesh Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (BCCSAP) 2019 is the key strategy document of the government of Bangladesh combating climate change. The strategy document includes six thematic areas: 1) climate change adaptation; 2) disaster risk management; 3) infrastructure, 4) research and knowledge management; 5) mitigation and low carbon development; and 6) institutional strengthening. The proposed RHL project is aligned with the thematic areas 1, 3 and 4. Additionally, it is aligned with the country’s National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA), which was approved by the government in November 2018.

The government prepared the Climate Change and Gender Action Plan in 2013 to mainstream climate change issues at all levels of planning and implementation. The proposed project will involve mostly women, who make up more than 80 per cent of the targeted community. Besides, the government of Bangladesh mainstreamed climate change issues in short-, medium- and long-term plans including coastal embankments and multipurpose cyclone shelters.

## B

### Crab related policy gaps

#### 42. The industry is taking preliminary steps in the right direction, with an annual historical increase in both demand and supply. While still in its nascent stage, the sector relies heavily on the collection of crabs from nature (wild), crab farming, and export. However, it also means that proper policies need to be developed in order to nurture the industry from its nascent stage into a sustainable one. The Government of Bangladesh under the Crab and crab-based production management rules 2019 (under the Wildlife Protection Act) has issued guidelines for crab collection from nature (wild), crab farming, and export. But there are still some gaps in this rule. Primarily, policies need to be developed to ensure that the crabs collected from wild habitats are not depleted, and that the types of crabs that can be collected from the wild in order to protect the population from depleting. Lastly, it is important for the Government to develop a policy or manual for the exporters in order to ensure quality and brand are not compromised in the international market.

#### 43. Breeding Policy: It is important that the Government of Bangladesh finalizes a particular window regulation for crab breeding. The current situation is such that every year, the government issues a ban on crab collection from wild habitats during the peak breeding season (March to May). However, every year, researchers claim this ban period is inaccurate, resulting in business losses during the peak export season.

#### 44. Wild Crab Collection: While there are policies on the type of crabs that can be collected from the wild, these policies have yet to be properly implemented. It needs to be prepared a separate policy or manual for the soft-shell crab farming industry as well. The Government should also prepare guidelines on crabs that are being collected from nature for the soft-shell farms before these crabs can reproduce.

### B (2). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max 1500 words, approximately 7 pages plus diagram)

#### 45. The coastal communities of Khulna, Bograhat, Sitakunda, Barguna, Patuakhali, Bhola, and Cox's Bazar are among the most vulnerable to climate change impacts in Bangladesh. These communities live in highly-vulnerable homesteads that put themselves and their livelihoods at risk during every monsoon season. The communities depend on fishing as a primary source of income for their stable land, and crops. It is not only life threatening but also financially catastrophic for these communities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 18

## Key Barriers to a resilient development pathway for coastal communities in Bangladesh

### Barrier 1: Lack of climate planning and implementation capacity among communities for adaptive action

#### 46. Although Khulna has implemented several settlement projects in coastal areas, the activities of the proposed project will not overlap as these are urban housing projects (undertaken by Khulna Development Authority, Green Climate Fund Project on Coastal Resilience, etc.) and Community Based Adapted Urban Development (CBAUD) Programme for Barisal City Corporation project). And understandably, vulnerable rural people do not have access to these projects.

#### 47. On the other hand, coastal communities are already facing climate change impacts, leading to more losses of productive lands, decreased land productivity and degradation of natural resources. Low elevation, high salinity, and frequent cyclones make them highly vulnerable to climate change. This results in the migration of working-age adults, leaving behind women, the elderly and disabled, who have limited resilience to climate hazards. Without adaptation, the number of people facing coastal inundation and migration will increase.

#### 48. The ideal state is one where rural families and communities can withstand cyclones and storm surges that are likely to become more frequent in Bangladesh due to climate change. This would mean less salinity and higher temperatures; and families are not forced to leave traditional villages for a life of poverty because of inadequate land and lack of access to finance. But to get to this state, there are key barriers that need to be addressed first.

### Barrier 2: Lack of access to credit in large capital expenditure

#### 51. Despite the increasing expansion of MFIs and other financial institutions, a large number of the country's adult population still remains excluded from formal financial services. This is particularly true for rural women who are often unable to earn a living on subsistence agriculture and wage labour. These communities suffer from a lack of awareness, and are often unaware that they have access to credit facilities or are not aware of the procedures and barriers to accessing financial services. As a consequence, they are highly risk-averse to investment and largely fail to adopt the means to minimise additional threats from climate change. They have the least capacity to adapt because their livelihoods are already under threat. The existing credit schemes are inadequate when compared to their needs, especially for relatively expensive investments in climate-resilient agriculture or other adaptation measures.

### Barrier 3: Lack of livelihood diversification

#### 52. Despite having the know-how and capacity to adopt and implement alternative resilient technologies and practices that would otherwise mitigate climate threats, upscaling traditional farming

# Barrier 4: Capacity, standards, supply and market access in developing a community-based crab hatchery

## Soft- and hard-shelled crab meats are in high demand both locally and internationally. Crab exports from Bangladesh have increased significantly since 2010.

### The major export markets for soft-shell crabs include the USA, Malaysia, Singapore, Japan, and Australia.
#### However, wild crabs are falling, posing great risk to the industry and to the livelihoods of coastal communities in Bangladesh. This is due to overfishing, illegal fishing, and kidnapping by bandsits, especially of women.

### The market often disadvantages the poor and women, who find it hard to obtain a fair price. Demand for crab, however, is growing both in domestic and international markets.
#### To maintain a sustainable supply of crab eggs, a hatchery is needed. This would provide an additional livelihood opportunity for coastal communities.
#### However, there are several challenges including the lack of extension capacity, investment shortfalls, and poor bargaining capacity of small-scale farmers lacking direct access to markets.

### The current crab industry suffers from a lack of quality and food safety standards which hamper its development, especially in export markets. There is a lack of training on good aquaculture practices, feed standards and activities that mitigate contamination and the spread of disease.
#### Additionally, there has been an importation of Artemia (a copepod used as feedstock for crabs) from Vietnam, which has a large supply and could limit growth in a community-based industry.

## Project Goal and Change Theory

### Therefore, if target coastal communities in Bangladesh employ weather-proof construction, have access to quality feed, and are provided with training on good aquaculture practices, they will be better able to support the livelihoods of the most vulnerable people living in these areas.
#### This project aims to reduce the risk to the lives and assets of the most vulnerable people living in coastal communities by providing them with the skills needed to adapt their livelihood options in the face of extreme climate change. It will directly benefit approximately 42,672 people in the target area who have traditionally been involved in crab farming.

### To make sure that all parts of the project are well organized at the beginning of the project, communities will be organized into climate change adaptation groups with gender equity to support and provide valuable local knowledge.

Risks to the project

73\. The project relies on the implementation of structures and the planting of trees and growing crops. All of these are vulnerable to extreme climate events such as cyclones and storm surges when they are at their most vulnerable periods. This is especially true for the planted trees, which will be providing them during their most vulnerable periods and provide training on care and maintenance of those assets to beneficiaries.

74\. Crab farming in PNG is in decline due to a number of reasons, with disease being one of them. Crab diseases that affect farm-raised crabs have not been identified, but it is essential that consistent monitoring and quality control are maintained throughout the project. This will be achieved by implementing monitoring protocols, training beneficiaries in husbandry and food standards, and maintaining research links to diagnose disease quickly so that there is no chance of an outbreak.

75\. There is a need for knowledge sharing within the institutions involved with only available to those who have received direct training. Such project knowledge must stay inside the institutions and be easily transferable to other institutions and communities if the project goals are to be achieved. By creating, gathering and disseminating knowledge products, this project will ensure that institutions, communities, and not just individuals, are building their capacity.

Assumptions

76\. Women will be able to participate as equal partners in the project. All activities that include beneficiaries will include women in equal proportions.

77\. Conscious selection will provide enough of an incentive for crab farmers and goat rearers to invest in their climate resilient livelihoods.

78\. Climate models provide an accurate indication of future SLR and cyclonic activity and ensure that humanitarian assistance is necessary for the future.

79\. Community groups have the desire to work together and address climate challenges together. All indications suggest this is true.

80\. Crab farmers will find and maintain market linkages after the project. Through training and the development of guidelines, standards and network, this project will create an environment that will make it simple to maintain market linkages and sell crabs to both in domestic and international markets.

81\. Knowledge creation and dissemination will lead to replication. PKSF will do all it can to make sure that knowledge of this project and best practices leads to replication.

## Theory of Change Diagram (Broken lines represent co-benefit pathways; heavy lines represent direct distribution, thin lines indirect or weak attribution)

### Co-benefits

- **Co-benefit 1: Decreased risk of assault on women**
  - Crab harvesting is a dangerous activity due to large and predatory tigers and the presence of bandits who prey upon women and girls while they are out in the forest. This project will help women and girls avoid having to enter the forest for crab harvesting.
    - In communal shelters, women and girls can be protected from violence during cooking, drinking water collection, sanitation, sexual harassment, etc.

- **Co-benefit 2: Reduced Urban Migration**
  - Vulnerable people from flood-and cyclone-affected regions are leaving their livelihoods and their communities due to the destruction of assets and loss of income. This project will reduce urban migration by addressing the push factors, namely the loss of and inability to develop livelihoods involved in this phenomenon.

- **Co-benefit 3: Growth of crab and resilient housing industries**
  - All communities in the coastal regions of Bangladesh are vulnerable to cyclones and storm surges.
    - This vulnerability is directly linked with the destruction of assets and loss of income, affecting livelihoods and local economies. The project will indirectly support local service providers who provide building, transportation and labour to benefit from this.

- **Co-benefit 4: Decreased pressure on wild crab populations by industry**
  - Wild crabs are being overharvested due to high demand and a lack of ability to create a full in vitro life cycle that is appropriate for poor communities. This project will transfer in vitro technology to

## B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

### B.3.1. Primary goal of the project is to develop climate adaptive coastal communities in Bangladesh through the adoption of climate-resilient livelihood options, technologies and housing.

To achieve this, the primary goals are:
(i) decreased risk of loss of assets and lives from extreme weather events;
(ii) livestock resilience to storm surge and salinity; and
(iii) improved climate planning and implementation by communities and local-level institutions.

### Outcomes 1: Decreased risk of loss of assets and lives from extreme weather events

Studies have found that the majority (80%) of households in coastal areas are vulnerable to intense precipitation, cyclones and storm surges, and coastal flooding due to permeable materials. To sustain the livelihoods, the proposed project will provide support to construct climate-resilient housing systems.

The specific outcomes include:
(i) construction of 15,300 houses based on the selection criteria stated in Para 126;
(ii) construction of 3,000 climate-proof houses. The EEs staff will carry out consultation meetings in the selected areas to select the beneficiaries. They will prepare a draft list of beneficiaries and submit it to

## B

**PROPOSAL FOR APPROVAL:** The PMU representatives from PKSF will visit to verify the beneficiary list and provide approval for the final beneficiaries. The same process will be followed for all the activities. The core structure of the house is made with brick masonry. The walls will be built in such a way that the shorter side will face the direction of the wind, which will reduce the wind exposure. Secondly, the side of the roof with the greater slope will face the wind, and the roof will not extend beyond the wall. Thirdly, the house will have an open space on one side to allow air to circulate freely. The roof will only be attached to the core structure and will not be supported by any walls, so even if the roof is affected by the wind, it would not cause damage to the walls of the house or the floor, and so when the wind hits the roof, the thrust would be reduced.

### 89. In coastal areas, salt-salinity contaminated water was used in the brick kiln mix sand and soil for making bricks. The use of this type of material will result in the contamination of the salt-salinity contaminated water. As a result, salts/mixtures remain in the construction materials. It absorbs moisture from the air during the monsoon season and releases it back to the atmosphere when the weather is dry. This results in the bricks not made with saline water or soil. Fresh water will also be ensured during the construction of the house. In addition, the use of tamarind (Tamarindus indica) and other plants that are resistant to salinity will be used from water. The use of tamarind (Tamarindus indica) and some weak acids in treating saline water are examples of this. The project will also ensure the use of these types of plants in the construction of coastal housing in Bangladesh, the post-cyclone Sid rehabilitation project of the Government of Bangladesh, and other projects.

### Description of materials

a) For reinforced concrete (RCC) building/housing: The whole frame structure (footing, column, grade beam, lintel, etc.) will be established.

b) If the building will be built by RCC, the exterior and interior walls will be made of brick masonry and finished off with cement plaster.

c) For semi-concrete houses: The foundation and all walls will be constructed with the help of brick masonry. The roof will be made of CGI sheet roofing with wooden or MS angle trusses. Finally, all civil works will be finished off with cement plaster.

d) CGI Roofing: A corrugated galvanized iron (GI) profile with a wind velocity of high speeds will be used in CGI (Corrugated Galvanized Iron) houses. The plinth will be made of brick masonry, PCC (Plain Cement Concrete) casting will be done on floor, and walls. The roofing will be made of CGI sheet.

### Activity 1.7 Tree planting

91. Trees around the roofed houses are an approach to making the house resident to the storm and tidal surge. Trees slow the wind speed of the storm, causing the least damage to the house. It will also improve the ecosystem and reduce the impact of storms and surges. The trees selected for planting trees on the roofs should be supported/protected ones. These have the capacity to reduce the impacts of storms and total surges. The tree species will be selected in consultation with the local community. The project will plant 100,000 mangrove saplings (trees) per HH.

93. The IEs will be responsible for ensuring tree plantations. The procurement of tree saplings will be done in close consultation with the beneficiaries. The CGI grant will be used for tree plantation activities.

### Outcome 2: Livelihood resilience to SLR/storm surge and saltwater intrusion

94. A large portion of the coastal population is highly exposed to climate change impacts due to increased sea level rise, storm surges, tidal surges, saltwater intrusion into freshwater sources, and coastal flooding. These pose a significant threat to agriculture, brackish aquaculture, and open-water fishing. The majority of them are poor or very poor (50% and 42%, respectively). The poverty headcount ratio in the districts of Upazila and Satkhira respectively are extremely poor (8%) whereas the national average is 12.9 percent. Gender inequality is also a major issue, with women having less decision-making power within the household and workplace and are expected to manage the household resources more efficiently than men. Women are also more vulnerable due to their lack of access to unpaid care work, and create a cycle that undermines their climate-resilient livelihoods.

## Output 2.1: Traditional farming practices climate proofed

### Activity 2.1.1: Construction of slatted houses for goat/sheep rearing

97. There is a scarcity of baseline information on the homestead-level goal and sheep population throughout the country, but it is observed that almost every household in rural areas rears either goat or sheep. The project will support the construction of slatted houses to house goats and sheep, making it a good option for poor households. Most importantly, women are mainly involved in goat and sheep rearing activities as they have more time than men due to their other domestic responsibilities. However, farmers often face economic losses through the deaths of livestock from floods and storms.

98. The RHL will support 20,000 HHS (30,000 beneficiaries) through sheep/goat rearing in slatted houses. The IES's staff will be responsible for selecting the beneficiaries based on the selection criteria defined in Part 1 of this proposal. Beneficiaries are those who have a good knowledge and experience related to rear livestock; and women-headed households and households with disadvantaged members will be given priority. The project will use data from the Bangladesh Demographic and Health Survey (BDHS) 2015-16 of the Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics (BBS-2017); and have access to homestead-level information on livestock rearing activities in the selected areas through the PMU. The beneficiaries will prepare a draft list of beneficiaries and submit it to PKSF for approval. The PMU representatives will visit the households to verify the data and submit the finalized list of beneficiaries.

The proposed project will provide financial and technological support for improved goat and sheep rearing practices, including the construction of slatted houses in homesteads to ensure a sustainable year-round income. The CCAG members will arrange for the builders to construct the slatted houses. Once the houses are completed, PMU staff will visit them for quality assurance. Then the project will provide financial support to the beneficiaries and train them on rearing practices as a member of the selected households. This will empower them by contributing to family income and reducing poverty in rural areas.

The construction cost per house is Tk 10,000 (US$25). The total cost for constructing one slatted house is Tk 37,500 (US$94), which includes the cost of materials. The project will be co-financed by GCF and CBFU; goat/sheep rearing will be financed from PKSF as loans.

The slatted houses will be constructed on raised areas at a height above flood level. It will be constructed with wood or bamboo, and the roof will be made with rust-proof corrugated iron sheets, which are durable and easy to maintain. The walls of the house can also be used to align the roof with the walls. Thus, the slatted houses will be resilient to climate change.

## Output 2.2: Community-based farmed crab supply chain creation

### 1. Crab farming is relatively mature in Vietnam and practiced by the Center for Education and Conservation of Aquatic Resources (CECAR) in coastal areas with natural conditions similar to Vietnam, particularly for crab farming. It is a tropical monsoon climate, hot and humid all year. The average summer temperature ranges from 25°C to 30°C, while winter temperatures range between 18°C and 24°C.

### 2. The project will establish 50 hatcheries, which will support a sustainable crab supply chain. The objective of the project is to develop a community-based farmed crab supply chain in Vietnam by growing a crab aquaculture industry in the country. Although the demand for juvenile crabs is high, the project aims to provide training and technical assistance to local communities on hatchery management.

### 3. The project will follow a "learning-by-doing" approach to develop capacity among hatchery owners and staff. The community-based farmed crab supply chain will generate knowledge and best practices on aquaculture and crab farming, which will be shared with other stakeholders such as government officials and other stakeholders, including policy-makers through workshops, seminars, consultations, and mass gatherings.

### 4. The project will provide training to the hatchery owners in the areas of hatchery management and maintenance. They will also receive loans from PKSF to develop their hatcheries. Each hatchery owner will be responsible for the creation of an additional 100 crab farms in the selected coastal districts. The project will also develop guides and training manuals on food safety and provide training to the various actors in the crab supply chain.

### 5. The project will establish 50 small enterprises of micro-crab hatcheries. The IEs with guidance from PKSF and in consultation with communities will select the hatchery owners based on the criteria established by the IEs for selection, including financial capacity, willingness to participate in hatchery management, operation, and maintenance. They will also be eligible for loans from PKSF. This loan will partly cover the cost of equipment and materials required for the establishment of a hatchery that is covered by grant funding from GCF. Total initial investment for a hatchery is estimated at US $18,000. Of this amount, 50% (US$9,000) will be provided as grants to the selected hatcheries and the remaining transfer costs and PKSF will provide co-financing in the form of a loan for operational costs. So, the GCF-IEP contribution is US $26,000, i.e., 10%. Each hatchery will require two staff. So, this activity creates an additional 100 jobs in coastal districts. Empowerment of at least 50% women for both training and crab hatcheries. The salary of the hatchery staff will be covered by PKSF.

## CRAB HATCHERY DESIGN AND OPERATION

### Hatchery Layout and Design

The hatchery will be designed to accommodate approximately 20,000 juvenile crabs per cycle, with a total of 6-8 larvae rearing tanks (5.6 ft x 5.6 ft x 4.87 ft), 2-4 treatment tanks (5.6 ft x 5.6 ft x 4.87 ft) for post-larvae and juveniles, and an overhead tank (5ft x 5ft x 5ft). The filter tank will be set up in a way that its bottom is at least 1 foot below the water level of the rearing tanks to ensure proper filtration.

### Water Treatment

The larvae rearing tanks, treatment tanks, and adult rearing space will be located in one section of the brick house and maintained at an optimum temperature throughout the year. The hatchery will have a chlorination system for treating seawater with chlorine, followed by dechlorination before it enters the filter tank.

### Current Planning

Currently, PKSF is planning to establish the hatcheries in Cox's Bazar to avoid the cost of collecting deep sea water. The water quality on Cox's Bazar coast meets the salinity requirement for operating crab hatcheries and has a depth of 15-20 feet with no tidal influence.

### Tank Connections

Every larva rearing tank and treatment tank will be connected to the filter tank by plastic pipe to ensure a continuous supply of oxygen. The tanks will have electric light heightening systems, air connections for blowers, and an electric light heightening system. A power supply generator will be installed in the hatchery house.

### Observation and Maintenance

A microscope will be used for observation with zones housed inside the hatchery to identify and diagnose problems of the zoea if they occur. The hatchery will have a separate room for living quarters, especially if it is far from the entrepreneur's residence.

### Space Outside the Hatchery

There should be space outside the hatchery for additional equipment and other purposes. This area must not be too close to the hatchery itself.

Activity 2.2.4 Technical and financial support to "crab farmers" (E stage)

The project will select 90,000 terraced level crab farmers (men or women) to produce export-quality crabs in the form of live shell, frozen shell, and soft shell crab for consultation meetings in the selected areas to select the beneficiaries. They will prepare a draft list of beneficiaries and submit it to GCF for approval. The PKSF representatives from PKSF will visit to verify the beneficiary list and provide approval for the final beneficiaries. They will be provided with the loan for purchasing juvenile crabs, which are reared in hatcheries. The juveniles will be rear them for 30 to 40 days before marketing them. The financial instruments used will both loans and grants. GCF will provide the loan for purchasing juvenile crabs from hatcheries, while GCF grants will be provided for other operational costs.

The project will promote fish-fish-crab (F-F-C) integrated crab farming and tree plantations in and around the ponds. The farmers will grow trees such as mango, banana, jackfruit, etc., on the crab pond-crab or ghar as well as fruit trees around the ponds. The trees will provide timber, fuel, and fruit, in addition to the crabs that will grow in the pond, leading to increased additional income for the crab farmers.

### 152.
Two types of crabs are exported from Bangladesh: One is a live crab (hard shell), and the other is a soft-shell crab. The export of live crabs is done by the local agent called "Fairs" who collects them at the depot or sell them to the local middleman called Fairs. There is an agent at the depot. The agent solicits the farmers for live crabs, buys them from the collectors and sells them to the buyers in Dhaka (the capital city), and the non-exportable crabs are sold again to the farmers and consumers. Here, centric-level buyers re-grade and replace them before sending them to the international market. The process of exportation is different for soft-shell crabs. In this case, the chain is a little different [Figure 6]. For soft-shell crabs, local agents buy small crabs from collectors or hatcheries at a lower price than live crabs. They sell these crabs to processors after rearing them in a processing plant. The farmers sell them to the processors after rearing. On the other hand, the collectors

### Outcomes 3: Improved climate planning and implementation by communities and local-level institutions

**Addressing climate change impacts at the community level requires specialized institutions. Local government institutions in Bangladesh mainly deal with regular development activities. Besides, there are specialized NGOs that have been working on climate change adaptation activities through various programmes. These organizations would play a crucial role in promoting climate change adaptation activities within their respective communities and will be supported by the proposed Climate Change Entities in the proposed working areas and enhance their capacity through training and practice workshops. The project will also carry out policy advocacy, awareness creation, and capacity building for local government institutions including area selection, beneficiary selection, and livelihood activities. 120\. The project will support **382,475** people through a set of selection and eligibility criteria as well as close consultation with local government institutions and community. Available government data will be used to identify the poor and disadvantaged in the project area. These beneficiaries will be selected based on eligibility and prioritization criteria. The number of households which are selected may increase with additional criteria in case of the number of interested and eligible households exceed the target number.

- a\. Those who live in saline-poor coastal vulnerable areas;
- b\. Priority to women-headed households and other disadvantaged groups;
- c\. Poor and Ultra-poor households (as defined in the Household Income and Expenditure Survey (HIES 2016) of the Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics (BBS-2017));
- d\. Per capita daily income is less than USD 1.90 adjusted with PPP as an alternative criterion of HIES 2016;
- e\. Those who are not receiving any support from other projects or organizations;
- f\. Those who have salt-affecting land particularly away from agricultural land;
- g\. Interested in participation in the project and adoption of the project-promoted technologies and practices;
- h\. Willing to contribute to the project through loan, cash and in-kind contributions, as necessary.

121\. Selection criteria for gothaes/reefers in stated local houses (Activity 2.1.1):

- a\. Those who do not have financial capacity to purchase livestock and have capacity to rear livestock;
- b\. Women-headed households and households with disadvantaged members will be given priority;
- c\. Poor and Ultra-poor Households (as defined in the Household Income and Expenditure Survey (HIES 2016) of the Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics (BBS-2017));
- d\. Have access to the salt land.

The selection will be based on the order of the selection criteria from among the targeted households in the project area. When the selection process reaches 90,000 beneficiaries, it will be stopped.

122\. Selection criteria for homestead tree plantation and saline-tolerant vegetable cultivation (Activity 1.1.2 and Activity 2.1.3):

- a\. Poor and ultra-poor people;
- b\. Having homestead land of at least 60 square meters;

Women-headed households or physically challenged people will be given preference.

The project will select 90,000 beneficiaries for tree plantations and 90,000 beneficiaries for saline-tolerant vegetable cultivation.

123\. Selection criteria for crab hatchery establishment (Activity 2.2.1):

- a\. Those who have the financial capacity to set up a crab hatchery through their own initiative;
- b\. Minimum or good educational and technological knowledge on crab or shrimp hatcheries;
- c\. Previous experience with microenterprises operations.

*This document defined extreme poor as the person having purchasing power parity (PPP) below 1.25 USD a day and PPP below 1.90 a day is called poor.*

*Please note that homestead land refers to land or properties by means of buying or inherited from father/mother or other persons.*

    - c. Have the ability to run an managed crab farming activities;
    - d. Poor and ultra poor households (as defined in the Household Income and Expenditure Survey (HIES) 3rd and 4th of Bangladesh, 2015-2017), and
    - e. Those who have financial capacity to construct homesteads.

The selection will be based on the order of the selection criteria from amongst the targeted households in the project area. When the selection process reaches 13,500 beneficiaries, it will be stopped.
### Selection criteria for a resilient homestead (Activity 1.1.1)
- a. Those who have their own homesteads at or above total flood level with high exposure to climate change
- b. Women-headed households and households with disadvantaged members will be given priority;
- c. Poor and ultra poor households (as defined in the Household Income and Expenditure Survey (HIES) 3rd and 4th of Bangladesh, 2015-2017), and
- d. Those who have financial capacity to construct homesteads.

The selection will be based on the order of the selection criteria from amongst the targeted households in the project area. When the selection process reaches 13,500 beneficiaries, it will be stopped.

*The project will form 3,200 groups with 25 (+) persons in each group for delivery support. These groups are called Community Climate Action Groups (CCAGs). The CCAGs will be formed by selecting 1 person from 362,475 selected beneficiaries to form these CCAGs. The members will be selected based on intensive consultations with selected communities on the basis of pre-defined criteria that include: a) potential for climate change adaptation and mitigation activities; b) willingness to participate in climate change generating activity; c) understanding on the local socio-economic context, and d) willingness to participate in the CCAGs. The CCAGs will be responsible for identifying the impacts of climate change and its impacts on their lives and livelihoods. They will adopt and practice climate-technique adaptation measures and share knowledge with other members of the community. The CCAGs will support services in groups in order to minimize the delivery cost and to ensure the participation and collective action of all members. The CCAGs will also be responsible for identifying the impacts of climate change on their lives and livelihoods. They will adopt and practice climate-technique adaptation measures and share knowledge with other members of the community. The CCAGs will support services in groups in order to minimize the delivery cost and to ensure the participation and collective action of all members.

The CCAGs will also look after community infrastructure beyond the project. Besides, they are responsible for identifying the impacts of climate change on their lives and livelihoods and the necessary consultations at the local level and select potential beneficiaries. They will prepare a draft list of beneficiaries based on the criteria mentioned above. The PMU of EE will verify this beneficiary list and provide approval for the final beneficiaries. Thus, the PMU of EE will be responsible for carrying out this activity.

The female headed households and other disadvantaged groups will receive priority while selecting the beneficiaries (refer to Annex 1 for detailed information on gender priority). As per experience of CCIP, the female headed households are more vulnerable because their income is low compared with male-headed households and a female head of the households. The female heads are more vulnerable because their income is low compared with male-headed households and a female head of the households. Their participation in labour force is also low. These limitations made them more vulnerable women in married households.

---

Activity 3.2 Prepare beneficiaries’ socio-economic profile

130\. A detailed socio-economic profile of the selected households will be prepared by IE’s before providing support. The purpose of the profile is to keep a monitoring record from baseline to after project implementation and beyond. This will also serve as a reference sheet for the scheme, schemes and subsequent interventions. The PMU, at the EE level, will provide necessary technical support including developing tools such as socio-economic profiles, household surveys, etc., with the help of GCF.

Activity 3.1 Arrange monthly group meetings on climate change issues for CCAG

The IEs will have field-level staff to directly coordinate with the beneficiaries. She will assist the groups in organizing meetings, provide training on climate change issues and other health issues. The meeting agenda is designed by the group. The group will take responsibility of addressing climate change impacts through the project interventions. They will decide who will opt what types of crops, livestock or fish to be grown. The decision-making process for the selection of activities will be shaped and carried forward by these group members. PKSF will co-finance this activity through in-kind contributions such as venue hire, stationery, etc., and out-of-pocket expenses like cost of meetings (i.e., staff costs), local travel, brown papers, register books, sign pens etc.

This staff will go to the CCAGs and discuss selected climate change issues. For example, climate change adaptation strategies for agriculture, livestock or fish farming, renewable housing technologies, and climate change and gender. The PMU at PKSF will provide technical support to IEs for producing posters, brochures, leaflets, etc., on climate change.

The experience from the CCAG shows that CCAGs are functional where they are engaged in financial services. Most importantly, the CCAG members are continuing most of the activities as they are getting benefits from the project (e.g., microfinance loans, agricultural inputs, enterprise loans etc.) by the partner organizations of PKSF beyond the project. This will ensure the sustainability of the CCAGs.

Output 3\.2 Capacity building and IEs and relevant institutions aspirated on project

Activity 3\.2\.1 Provide a training manual on adaptation technologies and cash value chain

The PMU at EE level will provide a training manual to deliver ToT to the IE’s staff on climate change issues and project management (IE selection process is described in para 165 to 168). Approximately 150 staff from relevant institutions will be trained. The training will be conducted by PKSF with the help of relevant institutions in addressing climate change issues at the community level. Besides, another training manual on climate change will be prepared to provide training to the CCAG members and traders across the value chain (i.e., farmers, fishers, livestock keepers). The PMU at EE level will also prepare a training guideline for the IEs on climate change adaptation strategies in agriculture, livestock and fish farming. In addition, PMU will prepare training modules for crab farming as well as crab nurseries. The project will also provide training to the CCAG members on financial services (e.g., microfinance guidelines, environmental and social management guideline, procurement procedure, accounting and financial management) with the help of guidelines from GCF.

Activity 3\.2\.2 Prepare guidelines on project management

The project will also prepare necessary guidelines including food safety standards, activity implementation guidelines, environmental and social safeguards, procurement, accounting and financial management. The guidelines will be utilized by the project implementing entities. This activity will use grant financing from GCF.

Activity 3\.2\.3 Organize training for beneficiaries and stakeholders

Each selected EE (IE selection process is described in para 165 to 168) will prepare a training plan to deliver ToT to the CCAG members and other stakeholders. The training plan will require approval from the PMU. PMU staffs will closely monitor the training sessions as per plan.

#### Activity 3.2.4 Organize training for IE's staff

- PMU will organize and deliver the training sessions.
- About 50 staff will attend within a period of 7 batches.
- The training will be conducted at the capacity of the newly recruited IE's staff.
- They will learn about climate change and adaptation as well as management of adaptation project implementation, including the identification of climate-related activities within the organization.

#### Activity 3.2.5 Implement workshops and seminars

- The project will give 20 workshops at the national and local levels.
- The workshop will include project inception, policy advocacy, project planning, quality assurance, monitoring, awareness sharing, training, capacity building etc., including a NDA, representatives from other relevant government agencies including Ministry of fisheries and livestock, Ministry of Agriculture (MOA), Department of Environment (DoE), Bangladesh Climate Change Trust (BCCT), Bangladesh Meteorological Department (BMD), Bangladesh Institute of Water Resources Development (BIWRA), Department of Environment (DoE), Bangladesh Climate Change Trust (BCCT), Ministry of Fisheries and Livestock (MFL), Ministry of Agriculture (MOA), Department of Environment (DoE), Bangladesh Meteorological Department (BMD), Ministry of Women and Children Affairs (MoWCA), Bangladesh Rice Research Institute (BRRI), Bangladesh Institute of Water Resources Development (BIWRA), Department of Disaster Management will be invited to attend the workshop.
- PKSF will send virtual representatives from the project team, including the PMU, IE staff, and other relevant authorities of the organization will nominate the workshop participants. Besides, IE representatives, IE staff or their representatives will also participate in these workshops. The cost for these workshops is 50 per cent of them will be per centenium participants.
- All the workshops and seminars will be organized with grant financing from GCF.

#### Activity 3.2.6 Organize exchange visits for beneficiaries and IE

- The project will organize 15 inter-community visits in vulnerable areas.
- They will learn from each other and be encouraged to adopt climate-resilient technologies and practices.
- About 300 participants including the CCAG members, representatives of the local government, and other stakeholders will attend these meetings.
- The CCAG members with the assistance of field-level staff of IE will discuss the exchange visits in their respective areas. After discussion, CCAG will nominate participants from them.
- The PMU of PKSF will guide the IE staff to facilitate the meetings.
- It is a type of in-country training and sharing of knowledge/technology for the government.

### Activity 3.2.7 Impact assessment of project activities

The project will sign MoU with reputed public universities namely the Chattogram University, the Khulna University, Patuakhali Science and Technology University and Bangladesh Fisheries Research Institute (BFRI) to conduct research on crab diseases, examine heavy metals in water, identify quality of food under implementation of same kind of activity areas). This type of visit will be helpful for the smooth and successful implementation of project. This activity will use grant from the GCF.

### Activity 3.2.8 Research and development

1. To ensure at the quality level, the project will use these laboratories to examine quality of food.
2. The project will carry out lessons that have been learnt throughout the period project. Relevant project officer for knowledge management of PMU will carry out the lessons and develop a booklet for publication. This booklet will be used as a reference material in designing their future projects, management of adaptation projects and measuring short- and long-term impacts of adaptation interventions. The experiences on climate change adaptation and building climate-resilient houses are to be documented in the knowledge documents. This will also contribute to strengthening the capacity gaps of communities and implementing adaptation projects in the country. This activity will use grant financing from GCF.

This activity will help to address Barrier 1 and 4 in the funding proposal. Because the guidelines and tools for implementation of adaptation interventions are not available, there is limited knowledge and understanding of effective climate plans and accordingly implementing the plans. On the other hand, tools and guidelines will help to implement adaptation interventions effectively. This activity will also contribute to strengthening the capacity gaps of the communities and institutions for implementing standard adaptation practices.

### Activity 3.2.9 Real time evaluation study of the project activities

Currently, there is limited knowledge and understanding of the effectiveness of adaptation interventions globally. Therefore, this project will provide a preliminary evaluation system of the project interventions to understand their effectiveness in terms of adaptation to climate change. Hence, the project will conduct an annual evaluation during the implementation phase and continue the effectiveness study annually throughout the period project. This will help generate adaptation knowledge as well as learning for future projects and programmes. This activity will be implemented with grant financing from the GCF.
