## A team of technical reviewers will be engaged in using their services when required to appraise projects.

These technical experts will review the project locations.

### The PDP/PC, after ensuring compliance to all fiduciary requirements, will submit the Sub-Projects (SPs) proposals to the Board for approval and then to the Governing Body for final approval. As Member of the Governing Body, the PKSF's Managing Director will present the proposal.

#### PKSF as EE will bear the responsibility of implementing the project in all aspects. The PDP/PC will designate a Project Implementation Entity (IE) who will be responsible for the implementation and monitoring of on-site monitoring systems. PKSF will adhere to Results-Based Monitoring (RBM) system to ensure results are achieved.

##### PKSF always implements its projects through its partner organizations, which is selected through PKSF's policies and procedures. All these organizations are pre-qualified and enrolled through a transparent selection process. The following five guiding principles will be applied to select the IE(s) and to guide IE’s activities:

* **I.** The project would adopt a strategic and holistic approach that targets clear climate change scenarios, and it would be implemented in a manner that is sustainable under all of the abovementioned scenarios.
* **II.** Any organization requesting finance must demonstrate how it will contribute – through community-level interventions – to advancing the skills and knowledge required to adapt to extreme climate variability and climate change. Organizations requesting funds for community-based adaptation activities, such as those described in section E.7.1 of this document, would be implemented. The climate change programme would preferably build upon the foundation of existing projects.
* **III.** The project would include community leadership and local government bodies while ensuring gender equality.

##### Emphasis would be placed on transparency, information monitoring and learning to ensure sustainability of the project under all scenarios described in section E.7.1 of this document. V. CCGAs will play a significant role in ensuring transparency and accountability of the project at various levels.

#### The project under implementation will be subject to monitoring both by the IE(s), PKSF as AE and PDP/PC (as EE). The monitoring framework is described in section E.7.A – A detailed Monitoring and Evaluation Manual consistent with GCF frameworks and policies: GCF, EGM Evaluation Policy, Monitoring and Accountability Framework for Development Effectiveness (MAFDE) and the GCF Performance Measurement System (PMS), to guide the monitoring practices of PKSF and EE.

##### The monitoring framework will be based on three functions. First, through monitoring by PKSF and PMU will ensure accountability of the IE(s) to deliver the outputs and outcomes. This implies that resources are used efficiently for the proposed activities. Second, monitoring will establish proper documentation of project implementation (e.g., progress, challenges, lessons learned, constraints, outcomes and impacts). Third, monitoring will help gather learning from the process. Since adaptation experiences are new in many countries, it is important to document what works well so that we can learn knowledge and subsequently to the wisdom for future actions. In short, the role of accountability is significant because continuous business learning becomes a core issue for monitoring at the outcome- and impact-level achievements.

## Fund Flow of the project:

The fund will be disbursed to IE as well as EE. The fund will be directly disbursed to PKSF from GCF. PKSF will receive the GCF Proceeds in the Project specific account held in a commercial bank in USD. In addition, PKSF will also receive the proceeds from the co-financing partners in their respective currencies based on satisfactory performance. The PMU will prepare an 'activity implementation guideline', preferably in English, for each activity and provide it to the IE. The PMU will also provide instructions for each of the activities. The PMU staffs will visit the field level activities and check whether the IEs have done any distortion from the assigned guidelines and instructions. If the IE fails to maintain the standard procedures, corrective measures will be taken by the PMU and the IEs will be asked to rectify.

Once, the corrective measures are done, PMU will recommend for reimbursement. The implementing entities (IEs) will be reimbursed in accordance with the activity implementation guidelines. The criteria for selecting for the IE is that they must have established offices in the project area. This office exists only for the purpose of disbursing the grant and credit funds to the project beneficiaries. The fund will be disbursed through PKSF's mainstream credit programme to the IEs (as they are also partner organizations). The PMU will operate the grant part and the credit officers will operate the loan part. This is the operational procedure for blending projects and programmes of PKSF.

GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL 3/0  PAGE 43

It is to be noted that PKSF will select at least 15 partner organisations who will be working as IEs at the community level. They will mobilize the beneficiaries, engage local contractors, and ensure project services for the beneficiaries. As such, they will pay to the contractors and other service providers of the local level under their supervision. The implementation of the project will be carried out by these IEs. The PKSF will review the documents and carry out field visits to ensure that the bills are duly prepared and submitted. Then spending amount will be transferred to IE's project account. By re-inbursement, we mean this process.

### Implementation arrangement at IE level

162. PKSF as an EE will bear the responsibility of implementing the project in all aspects. PKSF will delegate field level implementation of activities to the selected IEs through legal agreement. The IEs, by signing legal agreements with PKSF, will be responsible for the execution and monitoring of the project services. The work of IEs is always under monitoring of the EE. The partner organizations who are selected as IEs must have a sound understanding of the project activities and their implementation through PKSF's policies, procedures (for IE selection criteria see paragraph -165).

The participating entities will be responsible for the following at the field level: They will monitor the outputs, outcomes, and impacts of the activities as well as its impacts on the environment and society. They will also ensure gender participation at the organization and community levels. They will site specific monitoring reports to the EE quarterly.

164. The IEs will employ a dedicated monitoring officer who will report to the chief executive or senior officials, not directly to PKSF. The monitoring officer will be responsible for implementing the monitoring framework as envisaged in the project proposal and will produce quarterly activity monitoring reports based on the Activity to Output Monitoring (ATOM) agreed upon by both parties. The monitoring officer will undertake site visits at least once a month. The IEs will also prepare an Organizational Assessment Sheet (OAS) and impact-level monitoring annually based on the agreed Impact Assessment Sheet (IAS). The information from these sheets will be posted in the assigned fields of the IEs and the PKSF's online system as well.

165\. Selection of Implementing Entity: A selection committee comprised of PKSF's senior officials will be formed to select the implementing entity. It shall have at a minimum:

- Permanent existence of the organization in the project area.
- At least five years of experience in implementing climate-related projects or programmes.
- A good track record of financial transaction (i.e., at least BDT one crore, which is around USD 100 thousand).
- Must be extra ordinary, excellent or at least good as per PKSF's assessment using defined assessment criteria which include financial efficiency, economic efficiency, operational efficiency, growth indicators, financial strength & risk management, accounting & internal control system, social performance, human capacity and governance.
- Valid legal documents including registration.

167\. Organizational Criteria for Selection of the Level of Involvement in Money Laundering and Terrorist Financing: It is to be noted that PKSF has set criteria for periodic evaluation of its partner organizations. The criteria include financial efficiency, economic efficiency, operational efficiency, growth indicators, financial strength & risk management, accounting & internal control system, social performance, human capacity and governance. Each organization will be evaluated based on the performance of the POs. Based on the performance criteria, the organizations are categorized as extra ordinary, excellent or at least good. The organizations which have been categorized as extra ordinary will also be considered for eligibility. PKSF will ensure completion of AML/CFT due diligence with satisfactory results in the selection process for TCF and service providers.

168\. The selection committee shall select a short list of potential IEs from the above criteria on a short list of the potential IEs. Thus, the competitive process will be followed to select IEs for this project.


B.5\. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)


---
**Page 49**

169. Climate change is an important additional threat to the socio-economic development of Bangladesh. The country is in the process of switching from an LDC to a lower-middle-income country by 2025, which requires considerable investments in regular development interventions. Hence, the country is not in a position to build climate resilience and adapt to the impacts of climate change shocks. GCF funding is important to build climate resilience for the most vulnerable people.

170. The coastal area of Bangladesh is vulnerable to many natural disasters including climate change vulnerability. Sea level rise will increase salinity intrusion, sea swells, storms and cyclones which will lead to increased flooding in low lying areas. In addition, sea level rise will also damage crop agriculture, aquaculture and other livelihoods of the poor community. Establishment of a coastal buffer zone is one of the most important adaptation measures for Bangladesh as it requires salinity environment including land and water. If sea level rises, the brackish aquifer would be contaminated by seawater which would make the area unsuitable for agriculture. In addition, present system of crab fattening threatens the availability of crab in the nature. Once, crab hatching season is over, the crab catchers would not need to catch wild crabs. It will help protecting the coastal biodiversity and environment.

171. Most crab growers remain vulnerable to shocks from extreme events of natural disasters, climate change threat and long-term environmental changes. The project seeks from GCF US$ 42.20 million as grant for hatchery, capacity building training and building climate-resilient livelihoods alternative options, whereby enhancing vulnerability. PKSF and IEA with the support of GCF will provide training, capacity building and financial support for crab hatcheries, crab farming to 352,475 people.

172. GCF involvement is very important and critical in two ways: (i) climate change threat and long-term environmental changes are likely to increase the vulnerability of coastal communities and the poor people of Bangladesh. Coastal floods, salinity intrusion, sea swells, storms and cyclones will likely increase in occurrence frequency and intensity due to climate change. The project seeks from GCF US$ 42.20 million to address these threats; (ii) Extreme climatic related threats also require additional financial resource to increase the scale of climate risk reduction interventions. In addition, the project seeks from GCF US$ 7.79 million for promoting climate-resilient livelihoods and value chain interventions at an estimated US$ 7.79 million.

173. Reflecting the limited resources available to the country as well as need to be the trigger systemic change, it is proposed that a significant portion of the total cost will be covered by the GCF in the form of grant. The project seeks from GCF US$ 42.20 million for hatchery, capacity building training and building climate-resilient livelihoods alternative options, whereby enhancing vulnerability. PKSF and IEA with the support of GCF will provide training, capacity building and financial support for crab hatcheries, crab farming to 352,475 people as well as improve the methods and application of a good practice. GCF involvement will considerably reduce the cost burden on the country and will be instrumental in achieving the overall achievement of successful pilots and good international practice. As a result, the proposed investment will be highly beneficial.

174. The project will be financially mutualized both by the GCF, the PKSF and the community. The project seeks from GCF US$ 42.20 million as grant for hatchery, capacity building training and building climate-resilient livelihoods alternative options, whereby enhancing vulnerability. PKSF and IEA with the support of GCF will provide training, capacity building and financial support for crab hatcheries, crab farming to 352,475 people as well as improve the methods and application of a good practice.

B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)


---
**Page 50**
---

### 17. The project will seize the climate change related negative impacts as an opportunity for the coastal people. Crop farming requires a comparatively high level of salinity. The coastal area will experience severe salinity under the changing climate situation. Thus, coastal people will fully use the adverse impact to their advantage.

#### 17.1 PKSF has already established a large crab hatchery at a cost of $1 million USD under its technology transfer component of Promoting Agricultural Commercialization and Enterprise (PACE) project. Microorganisms used in this project are being transferred from Bangladesh to Vietnam. The knowledge generated from these interventions will be used in the proposed project under the GCF. All the technological support for the project has been already developed, and the expertise from Vietnamese experts is also in place; a group of local experts has already been groomed.

#### 17.2 PKSF has been implementing goat and sheep rearing in slatted houses for more than three years on the Chittagong coast. The project has used locally available materials such as bamboo (Bamboo) and bamboo (Bamboo). It was concentrated in very limited areas. The usefulness of slatted houses for goats or sheep is in the sense that it can be easily cleaned, which is a major problem with animals reared on land. This technology will also be explored commercially.

#### 17.3 The project interventions will be long run. Because it develops a profitable economic sector, it will use modern technology for the livestock sub-sector which will be resilient to climate change as well. It will include all classes of people in the society. The entrepreneurs will seek finance from MFIs and other financial institutions. The government will also provide support. It is expected that business, financial organizations and NGOs will finance the sector in the long run. The government will also continue its support for this project. The local government will be involved in the implementation of the project. The MFIs will also be financed by the local MFIs if it was explored commercially.

#### 17.4 The project will have multiple adaptation impacts on the coastal communities as well as contribute to the overall national economy. It will provide employment and income opportunities, and provide livelihood support to more than 362,475 beneficiaries. The project will support entrepreneurs at different stages of their business life cycle. This is in line with the government’s development objectives for scaling up crab hatchery. The PCIs and financial organizations will provide financial support to the entrepreneurs.

#### 17.5 After completing this project, it is expected that participants will be more resilient to any adverse effects of climate change in coastal areas. In addition, to meet their extended demand for livelihoods, they will be involved in other activities such as aquaculture and fisheries. The local government institutions will broaden their social capital and institutional linkage. It is also expected that the long-term impact of this project will be seen through the development of a new sector with additional services and thus, these IEIs will be readily available for the project participants to offer any kind of consultancy or advisory services.

#### 17.6 The next strategy will follow PKSF’s policy on project closure. Primarily, an exit plan is prepared for the project. The implementing entities will be provided necessary guidance on project closure. The project will continue its activities until the end of the project period and will not be closed before the completion of the project interventions. The IEIs will organize workshops at the local level involving local government representatives to ensure that all necessary publications will be published during the tenure of the project. An exit plan is presented.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

**187.** The proposed project will contribute to increased climate-resilient sustainable development. The proposed climate-resilient homesteads will secure lives and livelihood resources of the selected 13,520 households in coastal areas of Bangladesh. These homesteads are also a safe shelter for women and children when using community shelters (Details can be seen in Annex B). The poultry and livestock of the beneficiaries will be protected from cyclones and storms by planting trees on their homesteads. The trees on the homesteads to be planted will protect them from cyclonic storm as well as provide income. More importantly, these homesteads will also provide shelter for women and children in case of cyclone or storm. Thus, the dwellers of the resilient homesteads will have social values and honor by providing shelter to the affected communities.

**188.** The availability of crab in nature has already become a challenging task. Mud-crab, a salinity-tolerant species which is resistant and less affected by natural calamities and diseases, established in the coastal areas of Bangladesh. However, the number of mud-crabs available for collection is currently collected from nature. The availability of crab in nature is also going to extinct due to over use of traditional fishing methods and increasing salinity in the coastal areas of Bangladesh as well as protect the species in the nature.

**189.** The establishment of the crab hatchery and farming will create opportunities of sustainable employment for the people living in the coastal areas. The project will establish 50 micro-crab hatcheries in the coastal areas where the local people can use the crab hatching technology to produce crabs, and some other people will cultivate the crabs on their farm land. In addition, some people will get involved in transport of crabs from the hatchery to the farms. The project will also establish 500 crab nurseries. On average 2 fulltime workers will work at each crab hatchery. So, the micro crab hatcheries will provide employment for about 1000 people and the crab nurseries will provide employment for 500 people. Furthermore, some other people will be involved in transport of crabs from the hatcheries to crab nurseries. In addition, 900 people will be engaged in different levels of value chain of crab businesses. Furthermore, the project will also establish 100 crab farms where the local people can use the crab farming technology to produce crabs. The project will provide employment for about 2000 beneficiaries who have been proven as salinity-tolerant livelihood options for the coastal zone. The project will also provide employment opportunities in transport of crabs from hatcheries and nurseries, help them earn income, meet their nutrition demand and protect their houses from cyclones and storms. Thus, the project will provide employment to 362475 people which is expected to have a positive impact on improving well-being. The project will also support 90,000 beneficiaries in producing salinity-tolerant vegetables and fruits for food security of the coastal zone. Furthermore, the project will provide support for climate-resilient homesteads. The number of indirect beneficiaries will be around 277,000 people. It is expected that 75 per cent of the selected people will increase their resilience to climate change.

**191.** Women will particularly engage in homestead-based activities i.e., sheep rearing, homestead vegetable and fruit production etc. The project targets that at least 10 per cent percent of the households these activities will be women. The project targets that at least 10 per cent percent of the households these activities will be female headed). The project will put priority to the women-headed for supporting climate-resilient homesteads and other livelihood options in the coastal areas. Thus, roughly estimated 50 per cent of the direct beneficiaries will be women.

**Page 56**
---

### D.2. Parallels with potential (max. 500 words, approximately1page)

#### Potential for scaling up and replication

**192.** The project will address the negative impacts of climate-related negative impacts as an opportunity for the coastal people. Crab farming requires a comparatively high level of salinity. The coastal area will experience increased sea-level rise, coastal flooding, and storm surge due to climate change. Coastal communities will fully use the adverse impacts of climate change by the project interventions for their well-being.

**193.** The homeland of coastal people is highly exposed to sea-level rise, coastal flooding and storm surge. The project will address these negative impacts as an opportunity for crab farming. Crab farming provides livelihoods for the coastal vulnerable communities in the country. This is an innovative approach of making the adverse effects of climate change a positive one by involving the coastal people.

**194.** The project will design a model house that can withstand storm surge and sea-level rise during the birth of hoppers but not the whole homestead area. As a result, home yards inundate during monsoon, even when there is no storm surge or sea-level rise. Coastal communities will learn how to build their homes in such a way as to protect them from flooding and storm surge.

**195.** As an impact of climate change is rising salinity levels, the agricultural sector due to increase in the salinity level, the whole community should be involved in potential and sustainable land use and production. Crab hatching is one of the most promising livelihoods for coastal people in the project regions.

**196.** Traditional crab people are already engaged in crab farming. The juvenile crabs are collected from natural sources and they are fattening up crabs in traditional ways. The local people informed that they could catch crab in their nearby rivers (about 10-15 years back) but currently they have to travel 34 km away to collect crabs. It is a very long distance for them to travel. Providing technological support and training to them will significantly increase the production and minimize the cost.

**197.** The project will establish a crab hatchery in Assamapura (subdistrict) of Satkhira district. Collection of juvenile crabs from nature is damaging the ecological balance. Establishment of micro crab hatcheries will reduce this problem. The project will provide financial support to the local people through small loans to establish the crab sub-sector in the coastal districts. PKSF will nurture the activities through Value Chain Development (VCD) for crabs. Under the value chain components of the project, crab farming is one of them and it will be supported by PKSF through VCD.

**198.** The project expects that crab hatchery will rapidly be extended in the coastal community due to its multiple benefits. They will learn about sustainable new technology in crab hatching and farming through this project intervention. This will increase their income and reduce poverty among people. It will also increase their income earning. It is also expected that project interventions will create profitable business interventions at local, national and international levels which will help rapid scaling up of the project.

**199.** The PKSF expects that goat and sheep rearing in stilt houses in Assamapura (subdistrict) of Satkhira district will be extended to other coastal districts due to its multiple benefits. It will increase their income earning as it increases their income due to low risk and higher income.

**200.** The project will design a model house for the coastal people making it resilient to climate change related shocks. Because low-cost housing or traditional one cannot resist the critical climate sensitivity in the coastal zone. The proposed project will design a model house for demonstration. The design will consist of stilt houses with proper drainage system and other facilities that can withstand challenges of existing the housing system to make it sustainable. Hence, it is expected that other development agencies will also adopt this technology to make their projects more climate resilient and sustainable.


---
**Page 57**
---

Potential for technology sharing and learning

199. The project will generate information and knowledge on the effectiveness of proposed interventions and share with different levels of stakeholders through meetings, seminars, workshops and other informal interactions. This will enable the project to carry out best practices and lessons learned to disseminate the technologies and practices in other communities in the coastal zone.

Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

200. PKSF has been investing in research and development of hatchery-based crab farming for the last few years, and a number of hatcheries have already been established with the support of the project. Some are now in operation. Besides, PKSF has established 3 micro-scale hatcheries at entrepreneurs' level. These hatcheries will be used to supply seed crabs to the existing hatcheries and MFPs exist in the project area to finance the expansion of hatchery-based crab farming and business.

201. PKSF will implement the project through (EUs). They will have expertise on addressing climate change impacts, microfinance, and social inclusion. The EUs will also be able to work with entrepreneurs and resident households. This will create demonstration effects in the society. The government will also incorporate the lessons learned from this project into its policies for promoting small-scale fisheries and their microenterprise loan in promoting this livelihood. There is already an established market and it will continue to grow as more EUs are working with their micro-enterprise loan component.

Contribution to the regulatory framework and policy

202. Historically, the government of Bangladesh adopted a reactive type of policies and regulations to promote potential economic and development sectors. For example, the government adopted the National Fisheries Policy in 1985 after the completion of the first phase of the project. It is expected that the project will influence the policy makers by contributing to the export earning of the country. The project will also contribute to the creation of employment opportunities for women and men, economic activity and income of the crab sector would be noticeable to the government; they would take initiatives to promote this sector. In addition, the project will provide technical assistance to PKSF which will engage all levels of government officials through meetings, workshops and seminars to sensitize them on the importance of promoting small-scale fisheries in Bangladesh. This will influence the government of Bangladesh's policies and strategies for integrating climate change adaptation livelihood and resident housing in the coastal areas of the country.

Overall contribution towards development pathways consisted with relevant national climate change adaptation strategies and plans

203. It is expected that the project will influence several policies of the government. The major policies to be affected are: National Fisheries Policy, National Livestock Policy, National Aquaculture policy, national water policy, and jamdani (wetland) policy. With the growing economic development of the sub-sector, the government would take initiatives to promote this sector. This will also influence the export earning of crab. There is huge potential of exporting of processed meat of goat and sheep. Satisfactory production of meat and other products like milk, skin would encourage the government of Bangladesh to create favorable policies for promotion for production and trading of goat and sheep.

Transformational aspects of the project

240. Crab hatchery and farming using already safety affected areas. Private entrepreneurs will be engaged in this activity which will provide employment opportunities for women and men in the long run. Besides, PKSF through its partner organizations will continue financing in this sector based on needs of the enterprises. Existing transport infrastructure and linkages are already established as this business is being run with natural crab.

205. The entrepreneurs, local NGOs and selected Universities will enhance capacity in juvenile crab production, growing crab and promote trades in national and global market.

**Page 58**
---

206. The project will empower the project beneficiaries in terms of awareness raising, increasing income for their families and playing role in decision making of their families.

207. The resident households will influence government's existing and future housing projects in integrating climate change adaptation measures into their design. This will be done by involving them with the National House Building Research Institution so that they integrate climate change in their design.


D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) Environmental co-benefits

208. The project will also benefit the environment of the intervention areas. Presently, crab catchers and farmers collect crabs from the natural sources. The crab catchers informed that they do not get crab during the rainy season as the river water level rises and the crabs migrate into the river in Sundarbans, i.e., the iconic mangrove forest in Bangladesh, for catching crabs. It means, availability of crabs in the nature is decreasing day by day. The project will produce crabslets in the households to reduce the dependency on natural sources. This will also help to conserve and protect the crab in the nature and improve ecological balance. Litter of goats and sheep are used to produce good quality compost. It is also used to produce vermi-compost which is very important for improvement the quality of soil.

Social-economic co-benefits

209. Individuals will not only be better able to protect assets as a result of this project (direct benefit) but also it will provide security and well-being co-benefits to women and girls by avoiding the need to use communal shelters during extreme weather events, increased income of the coastal people and diversification in their livelihoods, improved access to education and health services, improved nutrition security, reducing the traditional factors that push rural communities to migrate to urban areas.

Economic co-benefits

210. The project will promote to spark growth in local secondary service economic activities, from transport to sanitation due to equipment maintenance and provision. It is anticipated that these secondary service providers will synergistically encourage local growth and employment, increasing local wealth and further development of the communities.


Gender-sensitive development benefit

211. Crab growing and fattening technology is very simple and easily manageable. Women can get involved in crab fattening and other activities of the project. PPSF from its beginning puts emphasis on disability inclusion and gender equality. The project will focus on women's empowerment as a key area. Like other projects or programmes, the project will focus on gender-based development so that equal opportunities are provided to both men and women. This will increase the economic capacity of women in the coastal zone and raise their voice towards their rights. Wages of male and female will be the same under the project.

212. Traditional food preparation work after livestock at household level. The project will select all women participants for implementing goatherdings rearing in stated houses. Thus, the interventions will provide them with an opportunity to earn income from this activity which is a traditional skill making. The housing component of the project will certainly reduce their physical vulnerabilities as they would not need to go elsewhere during the emerging season.

213. The proposed resettled households will be able to take care of women and adolescent girls during disasters because they would not need to go to community shelter. In addition, they can grow vegetables in their own plots which raised households that will improve nutrition of both male and female members of the households.

D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Bangladesh

214. In spite of many challenges including a huge population, poverty, climate change and disaster-Bangladesh is one of only two fast-growing economies in the world. Before COVID-19, the country has been maintaining around 7–8 per cent per cent GDP growth over the last decade. The poverty headcount ratio at national poverty line (NPL) was reduced from 45% to 20% between 1996 and 2010. Considering poverty level at USD 3.20/day, the poverty headcount ratio is very high through out the country. The number of poor people in Bangladesh has been reduced from 87 million (Source: https://data.worldbank.org/profile/bangladesh#indicator) Bangladesh has also achieved significant progress on MDGs and SDGs. For example, child mortality rate was reduced from 634/100,000 live births in 2000 to 197/100,000 birth in 2017; mortality rate of under 5 children reduced from 85 per thousand in 2000 to 35 per thousand in 2016; maternal mortality ratio increased to 120per cent in 2009 from 100per cent in 2010; per capita income increased to USD 191.6 from USD 781.2 in 2010; and GDP growth rate increased from 5.57 per cent per year in 2010 to 6.0 per cent in 2014. However, the country is still facing many challenges including poverty, inequality and unemployment mainly due to COVID-19 pandemic. However, this development is not equal throughout the country. The coastal region of Bangladesh is one such area where people are far behind from the development trajectories.

### World Bank Study (EACC, 2010)

215. A World Bank Study (EACC, 2010) estimates that under the baseline scenario, the damages and losses stand at USD 4.6 billion from a single cyclone and associated storm surges of a 10 years return period. The total damage is estimated to be USD 38.5 billion for all cyclones and storm surges over the next 45 year attributable to climate change.

### Cyclone and Storm Surge

216. The coastal areas are frequently affected by cyclones and storm surges and coastal flooding Nation-wide, 70.31 per cent households in disaster-prone areas have katcha (made by mud) houses and 17.44 per cent have semi-pucca houses. These houses are vulnerable to heavy rainfall, flooding and storm surge. The coastal area of Bangladesh is highly vulnerable to cyclones and storm surges (in the Sundarbans) [BBS, 2015]. This means, around 80 per cent per cent households are vulnerable to climate change induced disasters. In 2007, Cyclone Sidr affected 34 districts of Bangladesh and destroyed 55,667 houses completely and around 162,000 houses partially in the southwest coastal zone of Bangladesh. The cyclone caused USD 89 million damage to agriculture and USD 100 million loss to fisheries. Cyclone Aila in May, 2009 had damaged 500,000 houses (DAB, 2009) and Sidr in November, 2007 had destroyed 34,860 houses completely and around 100,000 houses partially. The damages are expected to be increased in future with the increasing intensity of cyclones and storm surges.

### Climate Change Challenges

217. Considering salinity intrusion due to climate change, crop and vegetable cultivation is facing growing challenges. In addition, the coastal areas are also facing a number of other challenges including water logging, soil erosion, etc., which are affecting the livelihoods of the farmers. However, there are some salinity-tolerant varieties including BRR dhan-47, BINA dhan-19, Black rice, and others. The area under these varieties is very small (around 200 ha) compared to paddy, less than 5 per cent. But most of the coastal land experiences more salinity, particularly during the dry season. This has led to a decline in the production of paddy and other crops. Therefore, there is an urgent need based production system for building resilience of the vulnerable community. In this context, crab farming is potential resilient livelihood for the coastal poor communities.

### Coastal Population

218. Thus, considering all these challenges, the findings of this project suggested that majority of the coastal population is poor, small and marginal farm families and shrimers workers. They build their houses with mud and thatch which are severely affected by cyclones, storm surges and high tides. These

people have to spend a significant amount of their earnings repairing houses each year. Hence, they could not come out from the economic poverty. The livelihoods of these people primarily depend on seasonal agricultural and wage labour in the coastal zone of Bangladesh. Cultivable land is shrinking due to increased sea level and expansion of salinity. Thus, agricultural production is hampered. The surface and groundwater in the coastal zone of the country is increasingly contaminated with salt water. Women are particularly affected as they have to spend a significant amount of time, particularly the women, collecting drinking water far away from their locality, spending a productive long time for this purpose. This situation will be further aggravated by the consequences of climate change.

The NAPA identifies priority activities to mitigate impact of climate change and most of the activities of the proposed project qualify as priority activities. Project No. 1, i.e., promotion of drought and flood resistant crop varieties is directly similar to Project No. 6, i.e., sustainable use of water resources and awareness raising on enhanced climatic disasters. The Government of Bangladesh under the leadership of Prime Minister Sheikh Hasina adopted the Vision 2021. This project is strongly aligned with national policies including: Seventh Five Year Plan of Bangladesh government, the Bangladesh National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) and Bangladesh Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (BCCSAP). The National Environment Management Policy (NEMP), and Sixth Five Year Plan (SFYP) of GBD body articulate the country's commitment to address climate change. This sub-sector will additionally help promote innovative and climate-friendly technologies that are suitable for small-scale farmers, which will be adopted by private enterprises. It is expected that the private sector will catch up with in the longer run, and private entrepreneurs will start investing in the sector, helping to create a base of various entrepreneurs at different stages of the value chain of crab production.

Once initiated by the funding stemming from this project in the long run, these activities will be financed by the local MFIs.

# Economic & Financial Analysis

## 239. The RHL project proposes implementing several activities to increase livelihood resilience to climate change in the coastal region of Bangladesh.

As the coastal areas of Bangladesh are the most vulnerable to climate change impacts and will be affected by an increase in these shocks, over time, the proposed project's activities will help address population vulnerabilities to these shocks.

The appraised projects' interventions are geared toward the most vulnerable communities in the coastal areas of Bangladesh with highest poverty rates and significant levels of social and economic disadvantage. These communities have limited access to resources that are essential for their assets and livelihoods. It is certain that without any external support, these communities will be unable to internalize the benefits from climate change adaptation activities due to lack of collateral or credit worthiness.

## 241. Financial Analysis was pursued on interventions that are expected to provide income increase for all beneficiaries. The analysis was conducted separately for all (05) activities (goal mean traditional livelihoods, goal mean modern livelihoods, and goal mean mixed livelihoods) under two different scenarios; one with RCP 4.5 (medium case scenario) and another with RCP 8.5 (worst case scenario), with an interest rate of 12%.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 67

## E

From a technical and financial enabling environment.

The government and donors have built several communal hatcheries that save crab lives; they do not address asset loss.

The crab sector is currently unsustainable. Efforts to develop a fully in vitro supply chain were initiated by the PFSF in 2016 which is still functioning well. A few private micro-hatcheries are also developing and producing juvenile crabs.

A paradigm shift would see national housing directives that incorporate sustainable homebuilding standards and finance options, as well as hatchery-based crab farming and trading. The project would accelerate the transition to sustainable crab farming and trading by replacing wild caught with hatchery-based farming.

PFSF housing programme will continue funding through partner organisations (PODs) in building resilient households.

PFSF will work with government to develop a conducive enabling environment created by this project.

## Core Indicator 6: Degree to which CCF investments contribute to technology deployment, development or transfer and innovation

### PKSF have initiated a small pilot programme for crab hatchery production.
- **Low**: All people have access to climate resilient houses/shelters; technological assets protect lives and assets in coastal Bangladesh. Crab hatchery technology (and financing) is available to any enterprise wishing to adopt this climate resilient livelihood option.

## Core Indicator 7: Degree to which CCF investments contribute to market development/transform at the sectoral, local or national level

### Current markets are based on the unregulated/unustainable supply of wild crabs.
- **Medium**: The crab sector reaches a certain maturity that includes people of all levels, is transparently supported and guided by institutions and fair prices are attainable. The investment in crab sector will transform the market by having a fully in vitro breeding facility to allow small producers to enter supply chain and fair prices.

The project will introduce new building design technologies for houses/shelters resilient to climate extremes, targeting 3000 very poor vulnerable families with homes. It will disseminate crab hatchery technology and establish hatcheries to promote production of crabs in the coastal areas of Bangladesh directly benefiting over 1500 people. PKSF will continue to commit funding to promoting technology transfer in these sectors.

## Core indicator B: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to effective knowledge generation and sharing processes, and use of good practices, methodologies and standards

- There is no general knowledge by everyone on institutions on crab hatchery technology.

### All levels of the supply chain:
- Knowledge is generated, compiled and disseminated sporadically through this project.
- The project will develop a tool to track good practices which will be refined through an adaptation methodology which will improve adaptation across the project and across countries.
- The project will generate quarterly monitoring reports, interim final evaluation report, quarterly newsletters, e-newsletters, annual annual studies under GMF process etc.

## E7: Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The monitoring and evaluation (M&E) under this project will have three functions. Firstly, ongoing monitoring by PMU will ensure accountability of the implementation process and that the resources allocated to the project are used efficiently for the proposed activities. Secondly, monitoring will establish proper documentation of the implementation process and achievements at different levels (outputs, outcomes, and impacts). Third, monitoring will help gather learning from the process. In short, the role of accountability is significant in the case of outputs, whereas learning becomes more important when it comes to outcomes and impacts. The PMU should ensure that the project activities are aligned with the objectives of the project, maintain efficient operations, satisfy internal and external reporting obligations, and inform future programming, project design, implementation, and completion. The PMU’s M&E system should always be aligned with the major stages of a project's lifecycle (see Figure 9).

Mainly, four types of monitoring will be done in this project using different instrument. They are: i) Programmatic Monitoring: This justifies the delivery mechanism of the project and monitors whether the deliverables are being supplied properly; ii) Budget

Monitoring: Budget monitoring refers to proper book-keeping of the account of the project. This ensures the transparency in financial matters of the project and its impact. 248. Monitoring and Evaluation will be done, at different stages of the project implementation, through following instruments or systems such as (i) On-site monitoring and evaluation system (ii) Review and audit reports (iii) Project Management Information System (PMIS). Monitoring is an integral part for any projects implemented by PKSF. The main question is: "Who monitors what to whom, how, when and where, and under what conditions?" Many times, the project management team may not be able to monitor all aspects of a project. Therefore, it is important that each EE has a mentor at the PMU level who will be responsible for monitoring the project on behalf of the EE. The EE will also have a mentor at the field level who will be responsible for monitoring the project on behalf of the EE and also as a link between the EE and the PMU. This mentorship system is in place to ensure that all aspects of the project are monitored effectively.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 91

## a) Base-line Survey: The independent consulting firm will perform a baseline survey at the start of the project to learn more about the existing state of the indicators that will be monitored throughout the project. It will be completed prior to the project's official start. It is the procedure or action plan for collecting data and information from participants in order to establish a baseline.

## b) Mid-line Survey: The mid-line survey, also known as a mid-term review or mid-term evaluation, is described as an thorough exercise typically held at the midpoint of a project. It provides a snapshot of progress made up until that point and highlights areas where action may be necessary in light of new circumstances, and their likelihood of realization. In the midst of the project, a mid-line study will be done. It will serve two immediate purposes: decision-making and taking stock of initial lessons from experience. Specifically, a mid-line survey will provide the project managers with a basis for identifying areas where action is needed to improve performance or take advantage of opportunities as well as reinforcing initiatives that demonstrate the potential for success.

## c) End-Line Survey: Following the completion of the project, an end-line survey will be carried out. The outcomes of the end-line will be evaluated in comparison to those from the midline and baseline results of the baseline investigation. The action will be taken as part of an impact assessment. By comparing the same individuals before and after the project intervention, an end-line research will be conducted with the aim of estimating the impact of a specific intervention on the target population. This is done by randomly assigning participants to either the treatment group or the control group. The RCT method, which uses one of the most scientific methods of impact evaluation of certain interventions is Randomized Control Trial (RCT), where programme interventions are placed randomly at the individual or cluster level. Recently, this method has been used in the context of social research. Impact evaluation using RCT is considered to be a gold standard because it provides unbiased estimates of treatment effects. The comparison between the treatment group with the control group provides biased estimate of the intervention impact. Selection bias arises particularly for two reasons:

(1) Interventions are not randomly assigned, and (2) Some participants may have been more likely than others to participate in the programme. In RCT method, interventions are placed randomly which remove the possibility of the selection bias and provide unbiased estimation of treatment impact.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (see pages)

### 202. The project has been carefully reviewed at social, financial, operational and environmental risks.

The environmental risks from crab hatchery and farming and house construction are considered low to medium. To mitigate Environmental risks, the project has already developed an Environmental Management Plan (EMP) which includes a detailed description of the potential adverse effects and magnitude of impact on the environment. These are Environmental Screening and ES impact assessment studies. The social risk is that small-scale crab farmers who have invested in crab farming may lose their income as crab growers will buy crab-at-the-hatcheries. These will be mitigated by involving local communities in the project, including women and youth, through a participatory selection stage, and the project design includes adequate safeguard measures to minimize this risk. The major operational risk is low probability, i.e., the reduction of coverage of beneficiaries due to currency exchange loss or domestic inflation.

The project has been screened based on the exclusion criteria presented in Section 4.3 of the annex ES. Project activities will not have any significant adverse effects on human health and safety. The project will have some impacts that will require some mitigation measures. For example, the project will construct a hatchery building which may cause noise pollution to nearby residents. Therefore, personal protective equipment including masks, hand-gloves, helmet etc. For reducing dust, the construction of the hatchery building will be done in phases and only one section at any time.

The project was screened based on the exclusion criteria of GCF’s ES safeguard policy. As per the screening, the project does not have any significant adverse effects on human health and safety.

a) The project will have small scale construction work like houses for vulnerable people as well as hatchery infrastructure. There is least possibility to become injured at a scale that requires hospitalization or medical attention. No one has been seriously injured in the past who was engaged in this activity. The project will not require any force labour.

b) The project will have small scale construction work like houses for vulnerable people with potential to increase soil and water salinity around the hatchery and crab ponds. But it will be implemented in already saltiness-affecting sites.

c) The project will have small scale construction activities like crab hatchery, resilient homestead, stalled houses for ghost-sheds etc. But the project will not require critical infrastructure like dam, embankment, road, bridge, etc., which may cause significant adverse effects on human health and safety assessment and safety studies.

d) The project will have some small scale construction activities which may include resettlement and displacement, land acquisition, and economic displacement of persons and communities. Moreover, there is no temporary displacement issue. The households/families will be living in the same houses as they have been living before the project implementation and will continue to do after another So. families/huts/has to be shifted from one room to another.

e) There are no significant adverse effects on cultural heritage areas.

f) There are insignificant ethnic minorities found in the selected project area. However, the project will avoid those areas where these people are living.

g) In the project area there is no land that contains historical, cultural, artistic and religious values or contains features considered as critical cultural heritage.

h) The project will not have any significant adverse effects on human health and safety in consultation with the affected communities. Besides, these will be implemented on beneficiaries’ own land to avoid any displacement of people.

i) The activities may involve women labour in the crab farm and construction of crab hatcheries. So, they may be affected by other local people or co-workers during the time to work place or while working at home. However, the project has already developed a communication protocol; the possibility of sexual harassment, abuse or exploitation is very limited.

254\. The project design has carefully considered all social, cultural, operational and environmental risks. The environmental risks associated with hatchery farming and house construction are considered low to medium. To mitigate Environmental and social risks, the project has already developed an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) for the project area. This plan identifies the nature of intervention and magnitude of impact on the environment. These are Environmental and social Sensitive Areas (ESA) and Risk Assessment. Some environmental and social risks are presented below:

### Identified environmental risks

- Climate resilient homestead development will require earthen works that will have potential to use top soil, which is a critical resource in Bangladesh. The project area has very poor quality of soil because it deprives the land of fertile nutrients. By gathering soil from waste land or using topsoil and restoring it later, such an impact can be avoided. Additionally, surrounding borde pits and ponds can be utilized for earthen works.
- None of the project activities will require cutting trees or forests. As the plinth of the homesteads will be raised above ground level, there is no need to cut down trees. The project area has a large number of wild crabs and thus increase crab stock in nature, which is the most significant impact of the project interventions on biodiversity.
- Dust pollution may result from soil handling during construction and more specifically, a lack of watering of the earth's surface. Such pollution is also a function of weather conditions—in the dry season, dust can be carried by wind for long distances. The project area has no major roads; therefore, construction-related activities are less susceptible to dust accumulation. Noise pollution is normally due to some construction-related activities.
- The project will require crab hatcheries and farming, which will require saline water. The hatchery will use deep sea water that contains more than 25 ppm. In addition, crab ponds will also require saline water. These two activities may cause changes in the present soil and water salinity condition of the specific sites.

### Identified social risks

Social risk may be arisen due to establishment of hatcheries because existing crab collectors may lose their income as crab growers will buy crab-lot from the hatcheries. The project is only undertaking minor interventions, such as providing training and technical assistance for the collection of crabs and setting out with the help of local labour. The recipients typically carry out the suggested tasks themselves. Construction activities are also undertaken by local labourers. The construction of homesteads, the establishment and the reconstruction of homes will all demand labour. Local resources exist for this labor. Therefore, no outside labour will be required for the project. Additionally, there is a very slim probability of engaging in illegal activities because the project area has a large number of indigenous people.

260\. According to the Bangladesh Population and Housing Census 2011, there are approximately 1.65 million indigenous people living in Bangladesh, which constitutes about 3% of the total population. Of these men and women, 50.2% are women. Every ethnic indigenous group in Bangladesh has its own unique tradition, culture, legacy, and sense of self. They also have a variety of ways to make a living, most of which are based on agriculture or fishing. The project area is home to the largest number of indigenous people who live where they dwell. Twelve coastal districts in Bangladesh's south-west are included in the project. According to the 2011 census, there were approximately 354,879 indigenous people living within these 26,047 of them, or an insignificant 0.19% of the overall population, are indigenous people. The project area is home to the largest number of indigenous people who live where they dwell in Bangladesh's Barisal and Cox’s Bazar districts. If any indigenous people comes under the project, necessary action will be taken according to the IPPF of the project.


---
**Page 103**
---

261.The project interventions are community based and will not require external labour. Hence, risks associated with violation of human rights, particularly women, disable and other ethnic minorities (if any) are very limited or absent from external source. But, there are some internal risks to engage child labour in project interventions particularly crab farming and sheep/sheep rearing where the children occurred in the family members.

As project interventions are community based, women will directly involve many of the activities as these will be implemented at their homestead and neighbouring areas. However, the risk is very limited or negligible. But the challenge is that if any pregnant woman is involved in the project interventions, she will be transferred to the nearest health care facility at the IE level; there may be female staffs who will have to travel frequently in the selected villages. These staffs should be provided with a vehicle and they must be accompanied by a male driver at all times. These female staffs would require to frequent field visits in the remote areas of the country. Thus, they may be exposed to SEAN. Financial risk may arise at the procurement and beneficiary selection stage, and the project design includes adequate safeguard measures to minimize this risk. The major operational risk, at medium probability, is the reduction of coverage of beneficiaries due to currency exchange loss or domestic inflation.

PKSF, having long experience in managing projects supported by donor agencies, has established a robust internal control system which includes financial and non-financial controls systems. PKSF has an established finance division headed by a Deputy Managing Director for Finance. The Finance Division is responsible for all financial matters of the organization. PKSF also have an efficient internal audit cell headed by a General Manager who directly reports to the Managing Director. PKSF has also implemented an effective risk management system which includes an efficient review procedures in coordination with floor level monitoring. An internationally acclaimed audit firm carries out external audit of PKSF every year, and will continue to do so for the same period that the project is being implemented. The Board of Directors of PKSF reviews all financial matters (please visit https://ksf.org.bd/kybdocuments/) .PKSF has also adopted a Whistle Blower Protection Policy. If any employee or other person who has information about illegal activities in the organization, the committee generates an investigation report with recommendation. The Managing Director of PKSF takes the final decision (about punishment) on behalf the offense.

## G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project is well aware about gender mainstreaming in the project activities. Hence, the proposed project has taken a proactive response and stringent approach to ensure that women are not only equally gendered differentially but also have resources and ability to pursue livelihoods and institutional support and capacity building, and this has fundamentally shaped all of the activities and outputs of the project. The project will work with women as direct beneficiaries and key agents of change in the face of a changing climate and resource constraints. The project will select 30% CRTS women who are currently engaged in the proposed project to be part of the proposed project. More than 50 percent of the total direct beneficiaries i.e., 181,237 will be women. It is already mentioned that 60 percent of the CRTS members are women and they are directly involved in the project activities. 80% of the CGI members will be women beneficiaries (among the beneficiaries under PICS). The proposed project will select 50% women as direct beneficiaries for the CGI component. The project will select mostly women because they usually teach their children at home. They will also teach their other family members about climate change and how to adapt to it. This will help them to spread climate change concepts and practices would to the next generation which will have long term implication of addressing climate change in this country. Besides, necessary female staffs will be enrolled at the CGI component to ensure that women are involved in all aspects of the project activities. Considering gender integrity, the project proposes more crab farming and slatted housing for women as they are mostly engaged in these activities. The total estimated cost for the beneficiaries also very high which is estimated USD 22.56 million which is more than 50 percent of GCF process.

Besides, CGI experiences showed that women were benefited both economically and socially due to engage them with CGI. In coastal areas, women are usually not willing to go to cyclone shelter during the cyclone season as they have no means to protect their livestock and other household shelter. Most important lesson was that they could talk about climate and disaster in their locality. They also felt that they were more aware of the impact of climate change on their livelihoods, especially rearing etc. We expect the similar outcomes in the proposed project. The CGI faced some challenges to engage women as they are mostly engaged in household chores and other activities in coastal areas were not much supportive due to shyness and hesitation. Besides, climate change was new issue for them. However, motivation through disseminating proper information helped to overcome this challenge.

The activities are designed in a way that the women will be mostly economically and socially benefited. The CGI component of the proposed project includes gold and sheep rearing and slatted housing. The proposed project will select only women participants for implementing the activity because traditionally they are more engaged in these activities than men. The CGI component also includes livestock animal including goat and sheep. But the traditional process of management is a constraint of achieving climate change adaptation. Therefore, CGI component proposes to provide technical support and capacity building training to make it climate resilient and sustainable livelihood adaptation to climate change.

The project design recognizes not only the benefits of women, but also considers the inter-sectional vulnerability to changing conditions, of those beneficiaries facing additional marginalization due to poverty, and social exclusion. The project design recognizes to build adaptive capacity in regards to

## Financial Management and Audit (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Disbursement and Funds Flow

271. The funds for the project will flow through a Segregated Designated Account (DA) to be opened by PKSF in a commercial bank. The disbursement is report based; i.e., an advance to the DA is made on submission of Quarterly Financial Reports (QFRs), including a forecast of expenditure for the quarter, and a statement of actual expenditures incurred during that period. An updated expenditure forecast for the subsequent two quarters having the balance available in the Designated Account will be submitted by PKSF at the end of each reporting period. The amount spent from the DA on eligible expenditures documented in project expenditures based on claims in the QFRs, and the advances to the DA is adjusted accordingly.
