# Secretariat’s assessment of FP206

## Proposal name: Resilient Homestead and Livelihood support to the vulnerable coastal people of Bangladesh (RHL)

Accredited entity: Palli Karma-Sahayak Foundation (PKSF)

Country/ies): Bangladesh

Project/programme size: Small

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

#### Strengths
- The proposal presents a good example of thinking ahead.
  - First, the project treats the climate-timed induced salinity intrusion as an opportunity rather than a threat and proposes livelihood option in response.
  - Second, it proposes building existing houses of the ultra-poor and poor according to a design that is resilient against present and future projected inundation levels.

#### Points of caution
- Replication and scaling up of the project will require external sources of funding, and the proposal does not present a strategy or plan for generating or mobilizing financial resources in the future.


---
**Page 114**
---

The proposal builds on existing investments and knowledge of PSKF and government on the development of the crab farming sector and resilient house designs for coastal areas. In support of this development, PSKF invested (with the support of GCF) in a feasibility study with its research partner from Viet Nam) in research on the suitability of crab production in coastal areas, including studies at crab hatcheries, and research into local productivity levels.

In terms of the resilient house design, PSKF developed the proposed design options jointly with the Bangladesh Building Research Institute and the designs will constitute a new building code for the coastal zones. The proposal presents a strategic linkage with government housing programmes targeting climate change impacts. Adoption of a new building code in this region is expected to be scaled up by PSKF's new housing loan programme, which complements the government housing programme.

The proposal represents a social protection measure as recommended by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) in its 2014 report. It supports the climate vulnerable ultra-poor, poor and women-headed households to access resilient housing and have a resilient livelihood option that will generate income and also provide security and resilience.

The Secretariat wishes to confirm approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the annexure and addendum XVII titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations", respectively.


## Summary of the Secretary's assessment

### Background
Bangladesh is a low-lying river delta with a network of more than 250 major rivers and thousands of tributaries and canals, with high population densities of 160 million people in an area of 145,379 square kilometres. Coastal zones are only 2 metres above sea level. Its geographic location, population density and low adaptation capacity make it one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change.


---
**Page 115**
---

### The vulnerability of coastal people is characterized in three ways: climate-sensitive livelihoods, limited access to safe drinking water, and high agricultural wage labor, which are highly climate sensitive.

The majority of the coastal population is ultra-poor or 'with', small and marginal farm families and others who depend on fishing and agriculture for their livelihood. Most of these houses, which are built of mud and goat plasters (leaves of an indigenous coastal plant), are severely affected by cyclones, storms surge and high tides.

Coastal inhabitants have to spend a significant part of the year in temporary shelters or camps during cyclone seasons, making it difficult for them to escape economic poverty.

The project aims to provide livelihood support to the vulnerable coastal people of Bangladesh (BBL) project aims to develop climate-resilient housing and livelihood options in seven coastal districts (Satkhira, Khulna, Pirojpur, Bagerhat, Kishoreganj, Cox's Bazar, and Chittagong). The project will provide shelter response to sea and tidal surge and intense salinity, where it is feasible to develop crab hatcheries.

The project addresses the following barriers:

(a) Lack of climate change adaptation capacity among communities for addressing climate change;

(b) Lack of access to finance to invest in large capital expenses;

(c) Lack of livelihood diversification; Limited capacity to adopt adaptation technologies; and

(d) Capacity constraints, including quality standards, supply market access in developing a community-based crab supply chain.

The total project funding amount is USD 49.9 million, which the requested GCF funds USD 28.6 million (approximately 57% of the total cost (TC)) as providing co-financing of USD 6.6 million in loans and USD 1 million in the form of a kind.

The project's environmental and social safeguards (ESS) risk is categorized as B.

### Component-by-component analysis

**Outcome 1: Increased risk of loss of assets and life from extreme weather events (total cost: USD 24.4 million; GCF cost: USD 24.4 million)**

* **What will be done:** The project will be implemented by construction of climate resilient homesteads for vulnerable ultra-poor and poor households, the majority of which will be women headed. The housing design is which is developed jointly with the Public Finance Research Agency, Bangladesh (PFRA). The design consists of a two-story house on elevated concrete plinths above coastal flood level. The design is also based on the building back better concept and incorporates climate-resilient features such as water harvesting systems, improved sanitation, homestead gardening, and tree planting. The activity will benefit 13,500 beneficiaries.

* **Additionally:** Homestead tree planting around the selected houses will be supported to contribute towards reducing coastal salinity. About 96,000 beneficiaries will receive tree saplings, including mangrove species.


---
**Page 116**
---

Outcome 2: Livelihood resilience to one-level rise/storm surge and salinity (total cost USD 19.4 million; GCF co-funding USD 12.8 million)

### Output 2.1 Traditional farming practices climate proofed

Under this output, the project will promote climate resilient home-based livestock and vegetable and fruit tree farming activities in the coastal zone. This will include promotion of agroforestry systems diversification of household incomes and nutrition. Main activities will include: building statistical models for predicting future climate change impacts; adoption of improved livestock management practices; and adoption of livestock feed varieties that could be grown locally, including drought tolerant varieties and the adoption of saline tolerant varieties and resilient farm management practices.

### Output 2.2 Community-based farmed crab supply chain created

Under this output, the project will promote community-based farmed crab farming because crab farming is tolerant to a wide range of salinity levels in comparison to paddy and shrimp farming as well as other aquaculture species such as tilapia. Current practices of crab farming are based on capturing crabs and crabs from nature with a negative impact on the environment. The project will promote farmed crab farming in paddy and shrimp farming as well as exploitative crab capture farming, to culture crab farming through investments into mini-scale crab hatcheries, upscaling farmers’ technical skills and promoting market linkages for the sale of farmed crabs. This is expected to lead to increases in the natural stock of crab species and mangrove plantations.

### Outcome 3: Improved climate planning and implementation by community and local level institutions (total cost USD 16.4 million; GCF co-funding USD 3.1 million)

As communities and local level institutions are often the first to perceive environmental changes, they lack related knowledge and experience. This component will focus on strengthening the capacity of institutions and communities to address climate change.

### Main outputs

To be achieved under outcome 3 will include the following:

#### Output 3.1 Climate change adaptation plans developed and institutionalized

Under this output, the project will focus on: selection of project beneficiaries based on socioeconomic profiling as well as technical assessments; particularly for the benefit of women and vulnerable groups; and development of a climate change awareness among implementing entities (IEs).

#### Output 3.2 Capacity among community and local level institutions involved in the project

Under this output, there will be development of training materials for adaptation technologies and cash value chain development; preparation of the project management plan; capacity building on climate change adaptation among beneficiaries and stakeholders (including training of construction companies and firms); and support to research and development activities including field-based research.

#### Output 3.3 Knowledge products prepared and disseminated

Project management (total cost USD 12 million; GCF co-funding USD 10.7 million)

The GCF portion of the project management cost is less than 5 per cent of the total requested GCF funding and is compliant with the GCF policy on fees


---
**Page 117**
---

# Implementation arrangements

The Palli Karma-Sahayak Foundation (PKSF) is the AIF and also the executing entity (EE). PKSF will engage with local environmental organizations which will serve as IEs responsible for working with the communities. The IEs will be selected by a Selection Committee established for the project, and based on the following selection criteria.

- **IEs must:**
  - Permanent existence of the organization in the project areas;
  - At least five years of experience in implementing climate change-related projects or programs;
  - A good track record of financial soundness (at least Bangladeshi taka [BDT]) 1 crore annually for the last three years;
  - Must be extremely excellent, at least good, per PKSF's assessment using defined assessment criteria which include financial efficiency, economic efficiency, operational efficiency growth indicators, financial strength and risk management, accounting and internal control systems, and organizational structure and performance; and
  - Valid legal documents including registration. Organizations will be ineligible on the grounds of involvement in money laundering and terrorist financing.

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### Impact potential Scale: N/A

The project will directly benefit 302,475 climate, economically vulnerable beneficiaries of which 50 per cent will be female. Additionally, it will support 178,665 indirect beneficiaries, of which 50 per cent will be female.

Through the climate resilient homestead project, the project will secure lives and livelihoods for 302,475 direct beneficiaries. Current government practices focus primarily on securing human lives through strengthening enhancements and building community resilience against natural disasters by focusing on water management, sanitation and hygiene facilities as well as the high risk of sexual harassment. Securing homesteads is a critical component in ensuring that households are not displaced or destroyed by protecting dwellers and household assets; the climate resilient housing activities would address risks faced by vulnerable populations.

The establishment of the crab hatcheries and farming will create opportunities of sustainable employment for coastal people. The project will establish 50 micro crab hatcheries in the coastal areas, which will support 12,600 direct beneficiaries. Each micro crab hatchery will support 30 crab nurseries. Fifty micro hatcheries will provide support to 500 crab farmers and their families. In addition, each crab nursery will employ two people. The micro crab hatcheries will therefore support 100 direct beneficiaries and 2,250 crab nurseries will support 20,690 crab growers. In addition, 800 people will be engaged at different levels of the crab farming value chain. This project will also provide technical assistance and financial support to 90,000 direct beneficiaries practicing sheep rearing, which is a proven salinity tolerant livelihood.

The project will support another 90,000 direct beneficiaries through tree planting around homesteads. This will help them to earn income, meet nutrition requirements and protect the environment. The project will also provide training in organic farming practices that will protect health and livelihood resources and lead to improved well-being. The project will also


---
**Page 118**
---

support 10,000 direct beneficiaries in developing saline-tolerant vegetable cultivation on their homesteads.

22. The number of indirect beneficiaries will be 770,058 people. It is expected that 75 per cent of selected community will increase their resilience to climate change and related consequences.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential
Scale: N/A

The project will promote paradigm shifts towards resilient and sustainable development pathways in the coastal zone by using salinity intrusion as an opportunity for creating resilient livelihoods and promotion of saline-tolerant housing and farming technologies and practices. The project will also contribute to the creation of a new value chain, where skills are built on PKN’s experience and investments in crab hatcheries and nurseries, and research on culture crab farming and saline-tolerant fodder and herbaceous crops.

The project will be implemented through several government policies. The project interventions will primarily influence national policy (e.g., the National Climate Change Policy, the National Water Resources Policy, and the National Land Use Policy) with the growing economic development of the subsector, the government will create a favourable policy and regulatory environment for the production and trading of crab. The resilience of coastal communities to climate change will be enhanced by integrating greater sustainability in the context of climate change. The project will share the results of its research on saline-tolerant housing with the Building Research Institute so that it will be able to integrate sustainability in future designs.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential
Scale: N/A

The project will address several crucial sustainable livelihood and resiliency-related issues pertinent to the coastal inhabitants of Bangladesh. Specifically, the project will employ interventions that are expected to contribute to (i) poverty reduction among the poor households; (ii) climate sensitivity of the population in selected regions; and (iii) scarcity of safe drinking water.

By providing protection for the individual assets of marginalized and climate-vulnerable households, such as housing and livestock, the project will also provide security and well-being co-benefits. By promoting the use of saline-tolerant crops and other agricultural practices and by creating new job opportunities in the crab production value chain, the project will also improve the quality of lives of poor communities, and their food and nutrition security.

By promoting the use of saline-tolerant crops and other agricultural practices, the project is also expected to contribute to the development of secondary service-economy activities with a positive impact on the livelihoods of coastal communities.

The project’s activity on crab farming promotes the shift from unsustainable shrimp farming and exploitative uncultured crab farming with expected positive impacts on the growth of farmers’ income and food security in coastal areas.

The project puts emphasis on disadvantaged, marginalized and excluded segments of society. Its investments primarily focus on livestock, horticulture and crab farming activities that are critical for their livelihoods.

The project will contribute to achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including SDG1 (no poverty: targets 1.1, 1.4 and 1.5); SDG2 (zero hunger: targets 2.1, 2.3, 2.4 and 2.6); SDG7 (affordable and clean energy: target 7.1); SDG9 (industry innovation and infrastructure: climate action; target 13.2); and SDG 15 (life on land: targets 15.1, 15.2, 15.3, 15.4 and 15.9).


---
**Page 119**
---

3.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: N/A

Bangladesh is one of the least developed countries, although it is scheduled to graduate from this status in 2028. The project will focus on selected rural areas, particularly the selected districts and upazilas that are most vulnerable to climate change.

Institutional needs are addressed through the training and capacity-building of the partner institutions. Capacity building for the local government and community members will support the project to achieve the target of increased resilience of beneficiaries and communities in the long term by providing necessary guidance on maintenance after the project ends. The project will also provide technical assistance to the local government at the local level.

The eligibility criteria for direct beneficiaries have been carefully designed based on the experience of PSKF and targeted beneficiaries will be selected according to this set of criteria. The selection criteria will be further informed and validated during the first year of implementation, with a focus on ensuring that the project is implemented in the management of target households' locations (proximity to storm/cyclone affected zones) to assess any additional metrics.

3.5 Country ownership

Scale: N/A

The project will contribute to the implementation of the Bangladesh Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (CCSAP), the National Adaptation Programme of Action, and the national communications, the Eighth Five-Year Plan and the Perspective Plan.

PSKF will work with at least 10 IECs that will work closely with the communities to develop awareness on climate change impacts beyond project closure. This is crucial in order to support appropriate maintenance.

Stakeholder consultation has been undertaken extensively, including with local community, government and non-government representatives.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: N/A

The project activities are tested and adapted to best practices and technologies to address livelihood options and increase resilience of communities. The activities build on the existing PSKF investment and knowledge base, with a strong emphasis on capacity-building for the local government and community members.

The project delivers public goods, for example through interventions in mangrove and climate-adaptive homesteads. It also delivers an increase in income through diversification of livelihood options, including livestock, poultry, fish farming, and production of high-value vegetables. The potential of reducing beneficiaries' economic losses is estimated to be 60 per cent.

IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

Project background: The project aims to decrease the risk of loss of assets and lives from extreme weather events, enhance livelihood resilience to sea level rise, storm surge and salinity; and improve climate planning and implementation by coastal communities and local-

# Environmental and social risk category

The predefined and assigned environmental and social risk category is GCF’s medium/category B, which is within the AEF’s accreditation level. This categorization is due to the actions which include renovations or construction of hatcheries, establishing new hatcheries, improving existing hatcheries, livestock rearing, and establishing crab micro-hatchery facilities that are expected to have risks and impacts that are generally manageable and low and specific, and which can also be readily managed with mitigation measures.

## Safeguard instruments

The AE provided an environmental and social management plan (ESMP) for the project. The document provides an overview of the national control for environmental and social risk assessment, potential environmental and social risks associated with the project activities, including environment and social impact assessment and management plans for subprojects categorized as medium risk during the project’s implementation.

## General environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards

The paragraphs below identify the key risks and/or impacts relating to the GCF ESS standards and how they will be addressed in the project.

### ESS 1: Assessment of management of environmental and social risks and impacts

Subprojects will be screened and categorized as low/minimal and/or moderate environmental and social risk. Subprojects with a high environmental and social risk will be managed by specific safeguard measures, including an ESMP. Subprojects categorized as moderate environmental and social risk will be required to have specific safeguards in place. These include the implementation of an ESMP, a monitoring plan for potential risks and impacts presented in the safeguards document. In addition, an assessment of the significance level of these risks has been provided, with most risks being considered low or minimal.

### ESS 2: Labour and working conditions

Construction-related activities under components 1 and 2, which include raising houses and livestock rearing barns respectively, have potential environmental and social impacts. These will be remediated by providing workers with personal protective equipment to wear during construction activities. The AE has also provided a health and safety plan for the AE in order to establish a process for assessing risks related to labour in supplying materials and mitigating them at the subproject level, including for solar power components.

### ESS 3: Air quality

Construction-related activities may create temporary noise and air pollutants for households and communities in the vicinity of such operations. The AE has provided an ESMP which includes specific measures on project sites to avoid potential contamination of water sources. Waste is expected to be generated from construction activities, crab hatcheries and livestock rearing. Additionally, the operational phase of the project will generate waste from the disposal of spent feed and other answer components, including excreta, after their lifespan has been reached. Maneuver from livestock rearing units may also result in the generation of dust. The AE has provided a plan for the treatment of saline water will be built into the hatchery design for the saline water required to produce


---
**Page 121**
---

### GCF.04.39/AR/01

#### ESS 4: Community health, safety and security - Construction may also pose a safety risk to households and local communities.

This will be mitigated by preventing access to rooms during construction and ensuring that the project does not use any food quality will not be used by the project. The AE is advised to also employ the use of signage in the local language to inform residents about the project, as well as providing training on hygiene and to ensure training for food safety and management for crab production.

#### ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement - The project excludes activities that will result in involuntary resettlement or displacement of households. Households will reside in other rooms of the house while the buildings undergo renovations to raise plinth, thus avoiding any loss of land. No compensation is required as per GCF policy. However, it may be required to implement project activities, such as the establishment of crab production facilities, that the AE will need to consider.

#### ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources - The project aims to prevent the collection of juvenile crabs in water sources by ensuring that all construction activities are completed before the breeding season. Juvenile crabs and crab nurseries produced in the facilities that it will establish. Any loss of vegetation from construction structures will be compensated.

#### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy 2017: Indigenous peoples - The AE has screened for indigenous peoples in line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and found no indigenous peoples within the project area. However, if any indigenous peoples are found during the project implementation, they will be provided an Indigenous peoples planning framework as a source of guidance to ensure their participation in the project’s development and implementation process. In line with its roles and functions, the GCF Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group (IPAG) is available to provide advice to the accredited entity and executing entities.

#### ESS 7: Cultural heritage - The AE has conducted an initial assessment which indicates that there are no archaeological, palaeontological, historical, artistic or religious sites considered as cultural heritage in the project area; therefore the risk is low.

### Environmental Social Health Assessment (SEAH) - Revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision B.120/2018 requires safeguarding from SEAH risks associated with the project activities. The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy programmes highlight key SEAH safeguarding in alignment with GCF policies, and the provisions outlined in this country’s policy document. The AE recognises that the assessment of SEAH outcomes is a continuous process throughout the project implementation, on the basis of PSEI’s first-level due diligence. However, it is recommended that the AE ensure prevention of SEAH risks through the identification and mitigation of potential SEAH risks associated with various SEAH risks associated with the project activities; The AE will engage different stakeholders throughout the project to identify and assess SEAH risks. A Project Management Information System (PMI) where any SEAH (and/or its equivalent) will be assessed and mitigation measures put in place. The PMI will also include a risk register, which will outline the identified SEAH risks and monitoring mechanisms in the event that SEAH occurs even though the AE’s assessment is that the risk of SEAH is low. The GCF SEAH safeguarding applies to all project activities, although women are the main focus under this project; the GCF SEAH safeguarding applies to all people who may be at risk of SEAH in GCF-financed activities.

### Human Resources Management - The AE will provide capacity-building training to staff of AE with roles and responsibilities for implementing ESS procedures, including screening. The PMI will have an ESS coordinator to guide the implementation of matters relating to ESS. The AE will also engage a third party to conduct site visits to monitor subprojects. A third party will be engaged to evaluate the

### Gender policy

The AIF has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan therefore complies with the requirements of the GCP Gender Policy.

- **Policy advocacy**: The AIF will advocate for gender equality through desk reviews and field visits.
- **Consultations at this stage do not include women focus groups, organizations and institutions.**
- **A detailed gender analysis is planned to be conducted during project implementation**, including consultation with the project beneficiaries and women and women’s groups, which will be done at the inception phase of the project.

The AIF has reviewed national policies and strategies to address gender inequality and women's empowerment. It has developed a women's development policy and nation action plan to ensure that women have access to education, health services, employment opportunities, engage and benefit equally in the rehabilitation process as well as getting access to necessary inputs. Like many countries, implementation and addressing the gender inequality issues are challenging. The AIF will work with the government to mainstream gender issues in climate change policies. As well the agriculture policy addresses women's role in food production and consumption, including their knowledge of food processing and nutrition, and also notes women’s contribution as producers and in value chains need to be better recognized. The AIF has developed a gender action plan that includes measures for addressing gender inequality and climate change on women and men. This along with the capacity of sex-disaggregated data has led to gaps in measures that address the differential needs related to access to land, finance, services, and inputs.

The gender assessment shed light on the linkages and differentiated impacts of climate change and particularly climate-related disaster on women and men. The gender assessment


---
**Page 123**
---

which were undertaken based on desk review indicates many differentiated impacts (and clearly shows the different roles of women and men, and the related challenges in mitigating and adapting to disasters and climate change). It for examples illustrates that mobility of women in Bangladesh is significantly lower than that of men, especially in urban or rural areas. Socio-cultural norms shape the perceptions of the value of women and largely designates unpaid domestic responsibilities to women such as household and care for the family. Women are often seen as weakly linked to productive assets. Their contribution remains socially invisible and has little exchange value thus reinforcing women's weak decision-making power in households. Women who do not own land or other property, have limited access to cash, livestock, and poultry, and may have access only through male relatives. Lack of access and ownership disempowers women economically and makes them socially subordinate. Women are often excluded from the decision-making process on household assets, limited decision making power, little access to paid labor and unequal pay renders women vulnerable, with no leverage to manage and control resources. Factors such as women having poorly represented in public decision-making structures or climate change policies, limiting their capacity to engage in political decisions that can positively impact their specific needs and interests.

59. Crab harvesting, which is one of the project activities, is quite a recent economic activity in which women are involved. It is also an activity where women have more flexibility to follow a full schedule that women have here. However, it provides valuable income while enhancing their ability to fulfill their roles as home-makers, and their roles and responsibilities within households. The gender action plan will be developed based on the findings of the gender assessment including the role of women in crab production and marketing. It will also provide access to innovative technology and approaches. These rules include specific tasks in the crab production process that are traditionally done by men or boys. Women are engaged in marketing, which could be a result of mobility issues and cultural norms that from upper caste communities. Lack of education and low level of education of women may also restrict their ability to adopt and use technologies.

The gender action plan provided is based on the findings of the gender assessment and includes specific tasks in crab production process that are traditionally done by men or boys, supervisor and report on the gender action plan. ### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

- This funding proposal is an adaptation programme to build resilient homesteads and provide livelihood support for vulnerable coastal people in Bangladesh.
- GCF is considering a grant of USD 42.2 million together with co-financing from PSKF in Bangladesh, which will be provided as a loan at a concessional interest rate and contribution. The co-financing ratio is 10:1 and accounts for 15.5 per cent of total financing.

#### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

- PSKF will act as both AG and AE for the project. As the AG, PSKF will be responsible for high-level financial management, including budgeting, reporting, and monitoring of the project as well as reviewing and reporting.
- In addition, PSKF will engage in day-to-day project implementation for the different components of the project. PSKF has a long-standing relationship with its partner, given its track record of over 30 years in poverty alleviation through socioeconomic development projects.

#### 4.3.3 Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

- The funding proposal's largest allocation will be for implementing component 1, which is the design and building of climate-resilient homesteads in selected coastal areas in Bangladesh.
- The execution risk is likely to be limited, given PSKF’s experience in building and reporting on similar projects. However, there are risks associated with the project, including development of the crab-raising industry. Crab raising is a nascent industry requiring high infrastructure investment and technical expertise. PSKF has gained considerable knowledge from its experience in crab hatching technology on a small scale.
- The next step is to replicate that experience at a larger scale.

#### 4.3.4 Compliance risk (medium risk)

- The project has controls to identify risks of money laundering and terrorism financing. During the implementation phase of the project, appropriate due diligence will be necessary to ensure that funds are used properly and that the project avoids violating sanctions or engages in prohibited practices.
- If there is a breach of these controls, it could have a significant impact on the project and the undertaking actions to reduce any significant risks to financial integrity.
- The project will have a whistle-blower facility for the reporting of any irregularities or prohibited practices.

Based on the commitments of the implementers and the anticipated risk profile for the programme in terms of money laundering, sanctions, or prohibited practices, the risk is rated as medium.


---
**Page 125**
---

### Project viability and concessionality

In the proposed project aims to build climate-resilient homesteads and provide livelihood projects for coastal communities in Bangladesh. The business models for climate-resilient buildings and most of the livelihood projects will rely on existing technology. Financing will be provided by GCF, with a loan from the World Bank.

The livelihood project on crop raising is the only aspect that will involve new technology, but this is expected to be viable, based on PSKF's experience. Required participation from GCF will represent 10% of the total cost of the project. The rationale for proposing concessionality level justified to help vulnerable coastal communities in the country adapt to climate change and generate significant adaptation benefits to the selected coastal areas in Bangladesh.

### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of results area and single proposal is not material.

#### Recommendation

It is recommended that the Board consider the above factors in its decision.

## Fiduciary

PSKF will act as the AE and the EE for the project.

As the AE, PSKF will be responsible for activities including but not limited to: (i) establishing the PMU; (ii) approval of and finalizing the project implementation arrangements, including procurement of goods and services, sub-granting, monitoring and evaluation, ESG standards and gender-related issues; (iii) facilitating financial flow to the project; (iv) submitting annual performance reports to GCF; (v) ensuring that the project is in compliance with PSKF's project management to draft terms of reference and advising on the selection of entities for implementation.

As the EE, PSKF will bear the responsibility of implementing the project at all aspects. PSKF will delegate field-level implementation of activities to the selected IFIs through legal agreements. The PSKF EE will ensure that the project is implemented in a participatory way along with communities to deliver the project services, and will be monitored by the EE. The partner organizations will be responsible for implementing the project at the community level which has been procured through PSKF's applicable procurement policies and procedures.

In terms of the flow of project funds, these will be directly disbursed to PSKF from GCF. PSKF will also receive a loan from the World Bank in US dollars. In addition to initial advance to EE from PSKF, PSKF will reimburse the fund to the implementing partners based on satisfactory performance.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP206

## Proposal name: Resilient Homestead and Livelihood support to the vulnerable coastal people of Bangladesh (BHL)

### Accredited entity: Palli Karma-Sahayak Foundation (PKSF)

### Country/ies: Bangladesh

### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Overview

- **Target country:** Bangladesh
- **Proposed project location:** The proposed project is located in the coastal zone of Bangladesh, targeting seven districts.
- **Type of finance:** blended finance (funding requested from the GCF is USD $42,000,200)
- **Context of vulnerability:** The geographical location and low elevation of the coastal areas make them highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. These areas are also prone to extreme weather events such as cyclones.


---
**Page 129**
---

will rise to 28-402 metres by the end of the twenty-first century. It is predicted for Bangladesh that a 45 centimetre rise in sea level may inundate 10–15 per cent of the land by the year 2050, resulting in over 35 million climate migrants from its coastal districts.* The proposed Green Climate Fund (GCF) project will focus on the coastal districts of Cox's Bazar and Bhola. These districts are particularly vulnerable to sea level rise, salinity intrusion, coastal flooding, cyclones, and storm surges because of low elevation ranging from 1–2 metres above average sea level.

6. The vulnerability of coastal people is characterized as follows: (i) poor human settlements; (ii) lack of access to clean drinking water; (iii) limited access to safe drinking water. The majority of the coastal population is poor, small and marginal farm families and稠iving in a highly vulnerable situation. They are mostly dependent on agriculture which is subject to coastal flooding. Most of the houses are built with mud and goat palai leaves (of indigenous coast plants) and are severely affected by cyclones, storm surges and high tides. These people have no access to safe drinking water and sanitation facilities in their homes year round. Besides, the coastal communities primarily depend on seasonal subsistence from climate-sensitive crops such as rice, jute, and vegetables which are highly vulnerable to sea level rise and salinity in the coastal zone of the country. The communities and local-level organizations have limited capacity to address these challenges effectively, especially in terms of adaptation, which is one of the key barriers to promoting climate-resilient development in the coastal zones.

7. Theory of Change If target coastal communities in Bangladesh employ weather-proof construction techniques for their houses, they will be better protected against cyclones and floods, and with the support of institutions, are knowledgeable of adaptive strategic planning and can coordinate with local-level organizations to address the challenges effectively. This will ensure livelihoods and futures into the future, because homes will provide protection during extreme weather conditions and food security in the face of climate hazards, and local institutions will provide appropriate and timely support.

8. Funding proposal outcomes, outputs and activities The funding proposal has three primary objectives: (a) to reduce the vulnerability of coastal communities to sea level rise, storm surge and salinity; Outcome 1 is improved climate planning and implementation by communities; (b) to improve livelihoods in the face of climate change; Outcome 2 is improved climate-resilient housing and livelihood technologies. The project will also enhance the capacities of communities and organizations in addressing climate change impacts in their locations.

(i) For outcome 1, the funding proposal has three immediate outcomes: Outcome 1.1 is decreased risk of loss of coastal homes due to sea level rise, storm surge and salinity (output 1.1). This entails the design and building of homesteads (activity 1.1.1) and homesteads with weather-proof construction techniques (activity 1.1.2).

(ii) For outcome 2, the project interventions are to: (a) promote climate-proof traditional farming practices (output 2.1) and the creation of a community-based farmed-crop supply chain (Output 2.2); (b) provide financial support for the purchase of livestock feed for goat/sheep rearing (activity 2.1.3), the provision of financial support for goat/sheep rearing (activity 2.1.4), and the promotion of climate-resilient vegetable production within homestead areas (activity 2.1.5). For output 2.2, the activities focus on

### 1.2. Project area

The coastal areas in Bangladesh where cyclone-induced loss and damage has mainly due to the increasing intensity and frequency of tropical cyclones in the target project area (i.e., Cox’s Bazar [29% of tropical cyclones], Sundarbans coast [Cuthia, Koyra, and Ghoramara] [18% of tropical cyclones], and Central coast [Barguna, Patuakhali, Pirojpur, Khulna, and Barisal] [53% of tropical cyclones]). The coastal areas are frequently affected by flooding, storm surges, and high tides frequently damaged more than two-thirds of the houses of coastal communities. These events have caused significant damage to infrastructure and agricultural resources (earning, savings, time) for house repair and rebuilding due to climate-related damage. In addition, these extreme weather events have also led to loss of livelihoods from extreme weather events via the construction of climate-resilient households (output 1.3).

### 1.3. Enhanced livelihood resilience of coastal communities

Similarly, tropical cyclones along with other extreme weather events such as storm surges and high tides have caused significant damage to agricultural landscapes and resources, in part due to salinity intrusion, and lead to environmental degradation. The total area affected by saltwater intrusion is 105,324 ha (Table 1). The total amount of salt-affected land in Bangladesh was approximately 0.13 million hectares (ha) in 1973, which increased to around 162,000 ha in 2000, and to an estimated 105,400 ha in 2005, and has continued to increase since then. The area of salt-affected land is now decreasing over the last few years due to several factors, and studies have identified salinity as the main cause of coastal erosion. In addition, cyclones and storm surges have reduced the resilience of livelihoods in project areas in the face of sea level rise and increased storm surges and salinity intrusion. The project will address these issues by promoting the creation of community-based farmed crab supply.

### 12. Enhanced access to finance

The project will complement a mixture of loans and grants that will help communities overcome otherwise inaccessible resources and sources of finance (barrier 2), thus addressing a major barrier to the development and security of coastal communities. The project will provide a loan facility for community-based enterprises in the form of loans and in-kind support. The loan will be used to implement activities under outcome 2 (activities 1, 3, and 4). In addition, the project will provide a grant facility that can be used to partially cover the project management unit (salary of the project coordinator) and office rent.

### Socioeconomic and gender co-benefits
Project interventions to support crab hatching, growing and fattening will create new business opportunities for poor members of communities in coastal areas. The project also aims at improving access to markets for small farmers, improving nutritional status: Crab fattening and improved sheep and goat-rearing livelihood opportunities will be provided to women-headed households and other vulnerable groups such as disabled households. The project promises to stimulate growth in local secondary economic activities in support of the new livelihoods, including transport, construction, maintenance and equipment provision. In addition, the project aims at providing shelter for people who are displaced or have to retreat to community shelters where their safety can be at risk.

### Water-related risks
Water-related risks (e.g., floods, droughts) pose a threat to the resiliences of communities and livelihoods against the impacts of extreme climate events(e. categories 1, 2 and 3), there is still the ever-present risk of major extreme events (i.e. categories 4 and 5) and sea level rise, which may have an impact on coastal areas.

Based on the above discussion, ITAP sees the sustainable development impact potential as medium to high.


## Needs of the recipient
Scale: N/A

### The target beneficiaries live in the coastal areas of Bangladesh (i.e. Khulna, Bagerhat, Satkhira, Barguna, Patuakhali, Bhola and Cox's Bazar). The housing situation in areas is mainly affected by cyclones and storm surges. In 2013, 78,319 households were affected, with 54% of households in disaster-prone areas have kitchens[constructed with mud], and 17.44 per cent have roofs made from bamboo or thatch. The most common cause of damage is storm surge as they are made with mud or straw or agipots (Nipa), a traditional leaf found in coastal areas. Cyclones, floods, droughts, sea level rise, and climate change are the main drivers of climate change and associated disasters in the country. The recent cyclone in May 2020 completely destroyed 55,647 households and damaged another 138,900 households in coastal areas of Bangladesh. Cyclone Mahasen in May 2013 damaged 150,000 houses, Cyclone Aila in May 2009 damaged 500,000 houses, and Cyclone Sidr in November 2007 damaged 600,000 houses in the coastal areas of Bangladesh. The increasing intensity of cyclones and storm surges.

### Climate change risks
The climate change risks facing the target beneficiaries, the coastal areas, polders, homestead gardens and small livestock cages are exposed to increased soil and water salinity, frequent annual flooding, sea-level rise, and tropical storms (categories 1, 2 and 3). This has a direct impact on food security, livelihoods, income generation activities (impacting crab production), loss of freshwater, and loss of livestock.

### The proposed project addresses directly some of these needs of the beneficiaries. It is believed that the barriers for the achievement are (i) lack of access to finance to invest in large capital expenses; (ii) lack of livelihood diversification; (iii) limited capacity of small farmers to adapt to climate change and variability; (iv) lack of sufficient market supply and market access for a community-based crab business value chain; and (v) lack of climate information services.

## Country ownership

The funding proposal is aligned with the Bangladesh’s Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan, national development plans (e.g., the Seventh Five Year Plan, Eighth Five Year Plan, and Perspective Plan). The proposed project is also aligned with various aspects of the concept notes prepared under the MIF.

Specifically, PSF is the executing entity (EE) and accredited entity (AE) for the project and has a vision of “a Bangladesh where poverty has been eradicated; ruling development and governance paradigm in inclusive, people-oriented, equitable and sustainable; and all citizens have access to basic services and opportunities for human dignified life”. PSF is a not-for-profit organization established by the Government of Bangladesh in 1996. It provides a wide range of social and economic development services, including financial, health, educational, capacity development, technology transfer and business support services. PSF has been working with government agencies, international organizations, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), and private sector institutions. PSF has a demonstrated track record in Bangladesh; it has established an Enterprise Development Programme (EDP) under the Microfinance for Small and Medium Enterprises Project; facilitated the inflows of climate change funds in Bangladesh as a least developed country development partner; provided technical assistance to government agencies on holistic development programs, an enterprise development programme, social protection programmes, capacity-building programmes, advocacy and knowledge management research and development. PSF has extensive experience in the field of aquaculture with particular experience in crab hatchery enterprises, especially in the management and production of crabs, having gained considerable expertise through its own operations as well as supporting PSF’s understanding of the crab hatchery technology needed in terms of water management (installation of aerators, etc.) and other aspects.

Extensive local stakeholder engagement. Local coastal stakeholders (e.g., local governments, NGOs and communities) have been closely consulted to identify local issues, challenges and opportunities for sustainable development. The project concept has been developed through rigorous review by key organizations (i.e., Department of Environment, Department of Fisheries, Bangladesh Agricultural University, United Nations Development Programme, International Center for Climate Change and Development, Action Aid and the World Food Programme).

### Strong complementarity of funding proposal with GCF projects.

The funding proposal optimizes the benefits of FP69, which provides potable drinking water for women who have to travel long distances to collect water. The project also addresses the technical benefits for the funding proposal programme on resilient housing and livelihoods (RHL). As per the RHL, the project will provide training in disaster risk reduction and resilience building communities into CAGs. The RHL project will apply lessons learned from the FP69 into implementation of the RHL project.

### Integrating lessons learned and best practices from other GCF projects

As per the AE, the funding proposal initiatives on goat and sheep rearing and resilient homesteads are also being integrated with the ECOFUND (ECOFund for coastal communities) and the ECCOP (ECCOP project); the RHL funding proposal is focused on southern coastal systems while the ECCOP Fund focuses on northern coastal systems. Given that the implementation of slatted houses will be similar under both projects, potential cross-learning opportunities from ECOFUND-ECOFund can be adopted for the RHL funding proposal, thus ensuring consistency and complementarity.

### Project conceptualization

The project's conceptualization is rationalized through: (i) the project's focus on a range of interventions that are climate-resilient (e.g. goat rearing and climate-adaptive homesteads); (ii) the delivery of interventions to communities that might initially be private in nature (e.g. funding of hatcheries for crab/crab fattening or of improved production systems for fish farming); and (iii) the potential for some of these interventions to co-benefit, might become public in nature.

### Rationale

The rationale presented by the AE, the analysis of the planned investments under three scenarios was conducted separately for all five activities (i.e. goat rearing, traditional vegetable farming, crab fattening, crab nursing and crab fattening). One scenario was considered as the best case scenario (B.C.S), another as worst case scenario (W.C.S) and another used BCP R.S (worst case scenario) (both of these scenarios were assessed with and without the inclusion of a MOU/MOA between PSF and AE, which is referred to as the MOU case). All the components under benefits and costs have also been modeled for all scenarios.27

### Impact on net financial present value (NPV), internal rate of return (IRR) and payback period

The NPV, IRR and payback period were calculated separately for each scenario of B.C.S (both proportionate GCF grant and proportionate PSF loan), the respective NPIs are positive for all three scenarios considered (i.e. goat rearing, fish farming, crab nursing and crab fattening).

**Page 136**

### Resilient housing and livelihoods versus category 1, 2 and 3 storms

The design of resilient homestead and hatchery activities has been planned to withstand a category 3 cyclone and should be able to withstand a category 4 cyclone in certain areas. The mangrove protection is intended to prevent loss and damage from the frequent lower category storms (category 1, 2) to some extent; as per the AIE, some of these structures might withstand a category 4 or 5 cyclone.

The AIE is mindful that there is an unfortunate occurrence of a 'siren' in 25 to 30 years. In such cases, the resilient loss cannot be completely avoided because each adaptation practice has its own limitations and if the cyclone intensity exceeds these limits, it causes loss and damage. This is mitigated by the existing redundancy measures already put in place by the government and local communities and was very well demonstrated during the 2019-2020 cyclones. The AIE notes that the most recent cyclone (Mocha) on May 14, 2021, did not cause any loss or damage to the existing cyclone shelters, water supply systems (WSS), cyclone shelters, the timing of the cyclone (during low tide) and the location in the Bangladesh coast. The cyclone shelter was built by the AIE with a capacity for housing 750,000 people and the housing of these people in cyclone shelters. As highlighted by the AIE, "Bangladesh has been able to save lives through its cyclone preparedness programme that has trained 76,000 trained human resources to reach almost everybody with an effective early warning with a five-day lead time. The strong legal measures through the implementation of Standing Orders under Section 145 of the Disaster Management Act and the establishment of district-level service providers at all the way up to the Prime Minister's office, is the event of an issuance of an

### Cost efficiency

The total budget of the BHL project is proposed at USD $49.99 million, with GCF grant financing of USD $22.80 million (85 per cent of the total funding) and USD $7.71 million from other sources. The project aims to build resilient housing and livelihoods to deliver direct benefits to an estimated 360,000 direct beneficiaries and 200,000 indirect beneficiaries.

The efficiency and effectiveness of the project is assessed as medium to high.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### (a) The urgency of resilience measures cannot be overstated. Bangladeshis living in "kutcha" mud embankments are always at risk of the impacts of fast and slow-onset climate extreme events, including cyclones, floods, droughts, and heatwaves. They are among the most resilient housing and livelihoods as they are always the first casualties of the climate change-intensified extreme weather events.

### (b) While building resilient houses and livelihoods along the coastal zones where poor citizens have lived for generations is indeed an opportunity to increase the resilience of these coastal communities, it must be done in a manner that does not exacerbate existing vulnerabilities at ever-present risk that could immediately wipe out the lives, properties and development gains in the project area. The recent category 5 Cyclone Mocha has demonstrated the importance of preparedness and response, as demonstrated in the very recent category 5 Cyclone Mocha storm in May 2023, ITAP will continue to monitor this situation.

### (c) Conduct awareness-raising activities informing the project beneficiaries of the limitations of the project interventions versus the impacts of fast-onset (category 4 and 5) storms and slow-onset climate extreme events.

### (d) Conduct risk mapping as the basis for building resilient housing and livelihoods;

### (e) Conduct regular disaster preparation and evacuation drills targeting the funding project beneficiaries. The ITAP recommends that these drills be conducted in conjunction with community drills on category 4/5 storm evacuation and timely and effective dissemination of category 4/5 storms (F-3; warnings as early as 5 days before impact).

### (f) Use remote sensing, geographic information systems, and climate-proof wireless technology infrastructure against wind and intense precipitation, and integrate climatological data information and disseminate it via other wireless technology infrastructure.

### The funding proposal is endorsed by ITAP.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP206)

## Proposal name:
Resilient Homestead and Livelihood support to the vulnerable coastal people of Bangladesh (RHL)

## Accredited entity:
Palli Karma-Sahayak Foundation (PKSF)

## Country/ies:
Bangladesh

## Project/programme size:
Small

### Impact potential
The AE appreciates ITAP Assessment, especially for duly acknowledging particular strengths of the project against the exigency of vulnerable communities in the coastal zone of Bangladesh. The AE also extends its deep appreciation for highlighting a few relatively weaker points, with recommendations to improve upon during the course of the implementation phase. This will ensure that the project is more robust and sustainable.

In addition to reduction of L&D and increased resilience of selected communities, the AE affirms that the project will enhance capacity of 3 public universities and 1 research institutes by supporting their laboratory-based research and development of crab.

### Paradigm shift potential
Additional to ITAP assessment, the project has plan to engage public universities and research institutions in crab research and development for future capacity building of the institution. The project also plans to establish a crab research and development hub at Moll with several (public financial) universities located in Chattagram, Khulna, Patuakhali and also the Bangabandhu Sheikh Mujib Medical University. This will be done through joint research focusing identification of crab diseases, breeding, production efficiency etc. This will extend the support services to the crab farmers in a sustainable manner, while contributing to the national economy.

### Sustainable development potential
In terms of sustainable development potential, the environmental co-benefits of the project would be significant. The hatchery-based crab farming will reduce the number of people currently engaged in wild crab fishing and thus the dependency on supply of crabs from outside the natural system. Free extraction of naturally available crab stock will be reduced significantly. It will, in turn, increase mud-crab stock in the nature, which is currently decreasing due to over-exploitation. This will contribute towards conservation of the coastal ecosystem and biodiversity. Thus the current rating on this investment criteria appears conservative.

### Needs of the recipient
No response

1. Introduction

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) recognizes the importance of gender considerations in terms of both impact and access to climate funding, and requires a Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan for all submissions.

2. An overview of the proposed project

Considering the vulnerability to climate change of the coastal people of Bangladesh, PNRF has decided to implement a project titled “On-Resident homestead and livelihood support to the vulnerable coastal people of Bangladesh (RHL).” A brief description of the proposed project is presented below:

Goal: The primary goal of the project is to develop climate-adaptive coastal communities in Bangladesh through the adoption of climate-resilient housing and livelihood technologies. The project will also enhance the capacity of communities and organizations to address climate change impacts in their localities.

The objectives of the project are:
1. To develop climate-resilient homesteads for vulnerable communities in the southwest coastal zone.
2. To develop climate-adaptive livelihoods for vulnerable coastal communities, and
3. To strengthen the adaptive capacities of communities and institutions so that they are able to plan and implement adaptation interventions.

Major activities

The project will directly involve 362,475 people; 715,000 more people will be indirectly benefited from the project. The total cost is estimated at USD 49,865,200 and the proposed period to 5 years. Major activities are design and building of homesteads, homestead tree planting, construction of slatted houses for goat rearing, development of climate-resilient agricultural practices, establishment of drought-tolerant vegetables within homestead areas, development of crab hatcheries, financial support for coastal farmers, training of local institutions and communities on adaptation technologies, and financial support to crab farmers. The project will also provide training to the local institutions and communities in identifying climate issues and adaptation technologies and transfer knowledge through practicing and documentation.

Monitoring and evaluation will be an integral part of the proposed project. It is expected that the project will contribute towards improved food security for coastal people; increase income, improve value chain system, improve ecosystem services and influence policies of the government.

Components of the proposed project

The activities, outputs, outcomes, results, and objectives are divided into three components such as 1) Decreased risk of loss of assets and lives from extreme weather events. 2) Increased livelihood resilience in coastal communities and 3) Improved climate planning and implementation by communities and local level institutions

Component 1: Decreased risk of loss of assets and lives from extreme weather events


---
**Page 144**
---

Studies found that more than three-fourth of the households in coastal areas are vulnerable to intensive precipitation, cyclone and storm surge and coastal flooding due to permeable materials. For sustaining the livelihood, the proposed project will provide support to construct climate resilient housing, including construction of houses with concrete planks or other suitable material above flood or tidal surge level; constructing/or reconstructing houses with concrete planks that are raised above flood or tidal surge level; providing training on adaptation techniques (e.g., coastal flooding etc.); construction of climate resilient sanitary latrines, rainwater harvesting system, homestead vegetable cultivation and other livelihood enhancing activities. This is very important for building resilience of the affected community; because they have to spend much of their income in rebuilding houses after cyclone or storm surge.

### Component 2: Increased livelihood resistance to SLR/storm surges and salinity

A large portion of coastal population is highly exposed to climate change impacts due to increased sea level, salinity in water and soil, intensity of cyclones and coastal flooding. These pose a significant threat to the livelihoods of the people living along the coast. According to IIPS, shows that 16 and 35 percent of living in Khulna and Sylhetic respectively are extremely poor whereas 20 and 48 percent of the population is moderately poor. Women play various social and cultural norms that shape woman’s day-to-day activities as well as their capacity to adapt to climate change impacts. They are responsible for managing the household and the workplace, and are expected to manage the household care and for the family Component 2 will provide support to women in building their resilience against climate change which underlines their climate change resilient livelihood. The proposed project will implement the following activities: a) capacity building of women on climate change adaptation techniques such as farming, homesteaded vegetable cultivation livelihood options and fruit trees and mangrove plantation. The elements of this component are: i) awareness raising among women about climate change; ii) capacity building of participants particularly women b) adequate and suitable access to resources for the implementation of activities c) provision of training on climate change adaptation techniques d) private sector engagement and improved climate change adaptation knowledge, attitudes and practices in the selected communities. The project will also provide support to the selected beneficiaries in promoting saline resistant technologies and practices particularly in agriculture.

### Component 3: Improved climate planning and implementation by communities and local level institutions

Addressing climate change impacts at the community level requires specialized institutions. Local governments in Bangladesh mainly deal with regular development activities. Besides, there are experienced NGOs who have strong and long-term relationship with local communities due to cost prohibitive nature of climate adaptation projects. The proposed project will select 15 NGOs as implementing partners for providing training and capacity building on climate change adaptation activities at community level. The proposed project will select at least 15 NGOs as implementing partners for providing training and capacity building on climate change adaptation activities at community level. The proposed project will select at least 15 NGOs as implementing partners for providing training and capacity building on climate change adaptation activities at community level. The proposed project will select at least 15 NGOs as implementing partners for providing training and capacity building on climate change adaptation activities at community level.

PKSF always works with poor and vulnerable people in group-based approach. For climate change adaptation projects, these groups are termed as 'Climate Change Adaptation Groups (CCAGs)'. One representative from each group will be selected to form a group of 15 CCAGs. The remaining participants together will form a group. The objective of forming this group is to deliver the support services and training on climate change adaptation activities at community level. It will also help transfer knowledge, skills and practices among the groups. This group will meet regularly to discuss climate change in a regular periodic interval formally typically monthly or in groups. Thus, they will be able to identify problems related to climate change impacts and will be provided with adequate training on climate change issues and how to deal with these problems. They will be able to identify climate change adaptation options and will be provided with adequate training on the impacts of climate change. They will also look after community infrastructures beyond the project period. Besides, the group approach includes the management cost of the project.
