3. Gender Assessment in the National Context

Although Bangladesh has made significant progress in poverty, human development and gender equality indicators over the last few decades, poverty and inequality remains prevalent, and the social status of women is still low. The country continues to face a high level of gender-based violence and a range of gendered inequalities, that is Bangladeshi women suffer under a particularly high burden of low-paid work, reproductive responsibilities, lack of access to education and health care services, childcare, and producing half of the food at the household level, yet making up only a quarter of the industrial workforce.

### 3.1 Social Aspects

The mobility of women in Bangladesh varies depending on social status, religious affiliation and whether they live in urban or rural areas. Social cultural norms not only shape perceptions of the value of women, but also restrict a large proportion of women to unpaid domestic responsibilities, further reducing their access to education and health care services. For example, 58% of girls are enrolled in secondary school, while 42% of women aged 15-19 were unable to attend a health center alone. The reasons for this include the lack of transportation (30%), fear of harassment (26%) and that when they travel other family members such as children (52%) or other female members (18%) usually accompany them, which is not allowed by social norms in many areas.

Although these social dynamics are in flux, and there have been important shifts due to economic conditions and the introduction of new legislation regarding the role of women in the household and public spheres remain deeply conservative.

Looking after children & old and cooking for all members of the family are seen as the central roles of a woman. The majority of women in Bangladesh work within their households, while men's labor performs is principally conducted within the premises of the household. This type of labor remains socially acceptable and economically viable. Women's unpaid domestic responsibilities further reinforce women's underrole in Bangladeshi society. The tradition of dowry still prevails, violence against women is widespread, and women are often excluded from decision-making more aware. Promisingly, a recent study carried out in the largest districts indicated a changing awareness among men regarding the role played by women, with women reporting that if they are financially empowered, they can do anything.

### 3.2 Gendered norms and vulnerabilities

Field visit report showed women's work continued from early morning to late at night. Cloth harvest times is the most critical period for women as they have to leave their paid labor work nor reduce their unpaid household work as both are important for family wellbeing. Unless women ask for help, men never join in with these tasks. The study also revealed that women who are involved in income generating activities face a lot of pressure and stress. Women fall under extreme time pressure which leads to both mental and physical stress and threatens their wellbeing. If women are away for work or visiting a relative's house, men find it difficult to provide care for the children and elderly family members as well as look after the home and children. If women fail to organize support, they can suffer mental stress and may have to cancel their time off.

Substantial numbers of women in the poorest groups in the project area engaged in income generating activities, mostly as agricultural laborers, but women from middle income and wealthy families are more likely to be involved in small-scale business. Women's decision making and choices are strongly limited by the patriarchal social norms. Even the women who are involved in farming and agricultural production are usually prohibited from participating in market-based transactions. This is because they have no access to credit, do not have their own land or selling and handle cash and thus have more assets.


---
**Page 146**
---

When women work as agri-laborers they are paid less than men. Wage discrimination is common in all the project areas, although the actual amount paid varies depending on the working conditions, workload, and approach of the employer. The limitation of women’s freedom of choice by patriarchal norms and practices also limits their access to resources and opportunities. Women who work as agri-laborers suffer both as a result of the natural disasters affecting agriculture, the main occupation open to them, and from male domination in the labor market, both of which result in disempowerment.

### 3.3. Access and control over resources and opportunities

Although women are more vulnerable than men due to their limited access to land and other complementary practices related to resource distribution and patterns of access and control over resources that are shaped by a history of gender inequality, they also face additional challenges in accessing resources because the poor and more vulnerable to the impacts of climate than men because of normative gender differentiated access and control over resources (ownership of land) and opportunities including education and health services.

#### 3.3.1. Land ownership

Land is usually owned by men, whereas those with more power as well as social cultural, economic, and political factors such as age, religion, and marginalized groups in the project area; women do not own land or other property, have limited access to other resources such as cash, livestock, and poultry, and may have access to but no control over agricultural land or homestead areas for vegetable gardening. Women are also less likely than men to be involved in decision-making about land use. Lack of access and ownership disempowers them economically and makes them socially insecure as resources become scarce due to climate change. This is further compounded by the fact that women et al., (2015) stated “People who do not have their own land for their own living are more vulnerable to climate change.” The field study found that women are discriminated against and deprived of ownership of family property and access to resources because of patriarchal norms and practices.

#### 3.3.2. Education

Education is an important component in enabling an individual to acquire skills and become empowered and develop the capability to adapt to extreme situations. Though girls’ access to education has improved over time, according to the National Sample Survey Office (NSSO) and Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs (MHA), 2015, the 73rd round of the NSSO’s Rural Household Survey and the 68th round of the Census (BBS, 2020) showed that in rural areas, male literacy rate is 73.29% and female literacy rate is 69.95%. Following the last census in 2011, the education rate for girls have increased as a result of government policies such as free mid-day meals, scholarships, and stipends to students up to grade 10, stipends for female students, and free distribution of national curriculum books from class 5-12 among others. The government has also introduced the National Literacy Mission (NLM) and compulsory education (http://indianministry.gov.in/department_all.php?dept=36). Nevertheless, the practice of child marriage is still prevalent in rural areas, which hinders girls’ access to education. Disasters also increase the difficulties for girls to access education; both physically – poor families are the most climate affected – and economically - loss of income, livestock, and dwellings can encourage parents to marry off their daughters early as a means of survival. This is further exacerbated by food intake in the family and ensure food security, social security, and economic security for their daughters. The field report also shows that girls are more vulnerable than boys to be affected by poverty and disaster, both which encourage early marriage and reduce school attendance.

#### 3.3.3. Access to services

The field visit report showed that poor women in the villages have less access to necessary national, and government-provided social services than men and wealthy groups. The majority of the people in the project area are marginalized, landless, and poor and thus unable to access government and banking services. Women’s limited access to these services is further compounded by their lack of confidence with dignity and women’s agency. For example, PKSF is providing support to about 1.5 crore


---
**Page 147**
---

3.3.4. Health

Health is another area where women are at a disadvantage due to cultural constraints, with problems worsened by climatic extreme events. Often, women need medicines they get it from the local pharmacy or through a doctor. For example, pregnant girls and young mothers who cannot afford services from a community clinic, which has limited services, rather than go to a hospital because of poverty. Villages are also not well connected with other areas by roadways. This makes it difficult for women to deliver because of the low cost and easy availability, and because married women prefer support and comfort at home during childbirth. In addition, in case of climatic extreme events (floods, droughts, storm surge), villages are often unable to move out the local area as a result of remoteness, which very much limits access to health services. Although government and other institutions provide safe and other medicines for the affected villages, these are not sufficient and not distributed equally.

3.3.5. Mobility and participation

Men have more freedom in mobility and mobility in village society. They can easily migrate to other places and make decisions without discussion, while women's mobility is restricted by the normative gendered roles and responsibilities. Girls and women are allowed to go anywhere outside of their homes only for specific purposes such as attending school or visiting family members. In case of extreme events, women are not allowed to move to another place to save themselves due to the lack of security in other places. Women also have limited access to credit facilities from banks or financial institutions because they do not have a formal identity and are considered to be under the guardianship authority. But due to restrictions on their mobility and the type of work they are allowed to do, women face problems in managing these responsibilities and in coping with extreme climatic events. In addition, women's participation is also limited by gendered roles and responsibilities in society including poor education, lack of skills, and lack of freedom of choice, and financial stresses become even more pronounced when the income from their work is reduced by disaster.

3.3.6. Power and decision making

According to traditional practice, men are considered to be the household head and family decision maker, while women, children, and other family members must obey their decisions and respect their choices. Women have no say in matters of family life or in the community because of the patriarchal system which men dominates, oppresses, and exploit women. Almost all the families in project area were headed by men and women had no say on any decision related to family life such as marriage, divorce, education, or financial status. Poor and marginalized women are even less able to take decisions on family matters and have very little control over their lives. Women's participation is also limited because of lack of skills and exercise their rights to power. The normative practice of a gendered hierarchy is for men to be the guardians of the family and to have the right to dominate women. The field study indicated that the key reasons why women are not involved in decision making at household level include poverty, unemployment, misconceptions about divorce, financial and social insecurity, and domestic violence. Women who participate in community activities such as training programs or workshops are more intellectual and socialized and have a better understanding about the outside world than women who do not participate in any activities. Women's participation is also limited because of the decision making process except as a witness to or attend cultural gatherings. Women can participate when there is a call from the union parallel on special issues. These restrictions limit development of women's mental capacity and their ability to make decisions, which results in women being less educated than men. This gender bias perception was visible even in accessing and using modern amenities like mobile phones, television sets, etc., where women are not allowed to use these facilities.

The government's initiatives on women's participation in the local general elections now. The women


---
**Page 148**
---

have access to open competition in local level elections as well as they have reserve seats at the local government structure.

Women are poorly represented in planning and decision-making processes in climate change policies, limiting their capacity to engage in policy decisions that can impact their specific needs and vulnerability. The UNFCCC Secretariat has highlighted the importance of incorporating gender in climate risk reduction efforts. In 2009, the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) recommended that national governments ensure that climate change and disaster risk reduction measures are gender responsive, sensitive to indigenous knowledge systems and respect human rights. Women's right to participate at all levels of decision- making is also enshrined in the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action adopted by the Fourth World Conference on Women in 1995.

In regards to women’s role in the domestic sphere, most household activities are done by women, with the highest participation in activities such as house cleaning, child care, cooking and meal preparation. Women also play a significant role in agriculture production, including rice cultivation, fish farming, tree plantations, dairy farming, and poultry rearing. Despite this central role in household activities, women's decision-making power is often limited. In many countries, decisions about the use of land are made by men, while women's participation in decision-making on climate change is still low, even in industrialized countries.

In Bangladesh, women’s participation in decision-making processes related to climate risk reduction is very low. The role of women in disaster risk management and adaptation is also limited. Women's participation in the planning and implementation of disaster risk reduction measures is often absent or minimal. This is particularly true for women who are involved in informal sectors such as small-scale agriculture, fishing, and domestic work related to climate risk including energy, transport, and urban planning.

Regardless, women’s central role in household management places them in a pivotal position for adapting to climate change impacts on food security and water availability. Given that women's responsibilities include managing water resources, their decision-making power is higher in areas such as food preparation and distribution, resolving food deficits and household work, which are directly affected by climate change. Women also play an important role in disaster onset impacts such as salinity and are assigned a higher responsibility in disaster preparedness particularly in coastal areas. ### 4. Position of Women in Bangladesh

The Constitution of Bangladesh (Articles 27, 28, 29 and 31) guarantees equality and non-discrimination on grounds of sex, religion or caste. Article 26(1) provides for the right to education for alternative activity in favour of the "backward section of citizens". Article 24 promulgated to ensure religious freedom within a pluralist, National Constitution and Article 28 (sections 1), 2 and 3 ensures equality between men and women. The law has made some progress towards ensuring equality between women and men in public domain but further scope for improvements remains in the private sphere. Bangladesh is a signatory to several international conventions on human rights. Some changes have occurred in some contexts, including in the situation of women. Efforts towards achieving gender equality are reflected in various national policies and strategies, including the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs), the CEDAW (1979), and the Beijing Platform of Action (1995), amongst others. Following the declaration of the UN Decade of Women (1975-85), the Government of Bangladesh has made significant progress in promoting women's rights. The country has undertaken several programs towards the advancement of women in the country. Simultaneously, the government has also been working on various fronts to ensure the resolution of women issues and enhancing women's participation in every sphere of life in order to achieve equality between men and women.

Gender equality is a goal that Bangladesh has achieved and the Government of Bangladesh also established institutions for girls and women at the secondary and tertiary level. However, concerns are raised over the high drop-out rate among girls, especially in rural areas; the general perception being that education is not important or necessary for girls. The lack of awareness about the importance of education, the fear of sexual abuse and harassment both at school as well as on their way there, Barriers experienced by girls to access education such as early marriage, poverty, social norms, the lack of facilities for girls in schools, the negative impact of early marriages and the lack of access to education for women are some of the major reasons. The government has also promoted equality of opportunity in employment and provided for equal pay amongst men and women. However, I observe that there is still a gender gap within the sector where the largest population of Bangladeshis work, which is being employed.

With regards to SDG-5 (Gender Equality), it is noteworthy to mention that the total fertility rate (TFR) has fallen from 7 live births in the mid-1970s to 2.3 births per woman in 2019 in the contraceptive prevalence rate (CPR) has increased from 4% in 1985 to 60% in 2019. The reduction in birth rate is also attributed to education of girls and more women joining the work force. Another important indicator that shows progress towards gender equality is life expectancy at birth which has improved from 57 years in 1960 to 73 years in 2020 from 46.7 years in 1960. Overall mortality amongst women of reproductive age has consistently


---
**Page 150**
---

declined over the last 10 years. The maternal mortality has decreased from 32 per 100,000 live births in 2001 to 17 in 2017. More needs to be done, however, to meet the SDA 5 target. At primary and secondary level education in environmental institutions, girls now account for larger proportions at 63% and 48%, respectively. However, at the tertiary level, the proportion of girls is only 39%, which is largely due to social reasons such as the marrying off of girls at that age. Overall, women's participation in the labour force has increased from 25% in 1990 to 34% in 2016 and Developmentments that it Bangladesh, a woman earns only 12 cents for every dollar that a man earns. This is one of the lowest levels among South Asian countries.

A major breakthrough has been achieved in the area of education and employment for girls due to affirmative action policies such as reservation in educational institutions and recruitment in government (RGMs) industries that employ mostly women. Although the wage rates at entry-level within this sector is much lower than in other sectors requiring similar or less skill, other issues such as unsafe working conditions, lack of social security benefits, etc., also reduce the contribution to women's empowerment and gender equality.

**Reasons of Gender Discrimination in Bangladesh**

Although many steps are being initiated by the government to address the problems of gender inequality, the improvement in reducing gender inequality is not satisfactory due to several reasons. Structural and Social Institutions: Traditionally, women were often discouraged from participating in public life and were expected to stay at home. The social norms and values that have been created over time are creating gender differentials, based on the age-old patriarchal traditions and values that still prevail in most of the parts of Bangladesh. Traditional perceptions about the role of women as homemakers still exist.

**Lack of Explicit Policy Initiatives:** There are policies to ensure women's security at home, education, health, employment, etc., but these policies have not been implemented effectively due to a grave concern. Under these circumstances, parents are more likely to keep their girls inside their homes. Hence, they are not able to participate in education, health, employment or other sectors. Therefore, the gap between men and women is widening.

**Preoccupied Mind-set:** There are perceptions that men are better off than women as far as the ability to work is concerned and only men can look after their parents. That is why girls are subject to discrimination from birth onwards. This preoccupation of mind set among people in general also creates gender discrimination. In the case of employment, the employees in Bangladesh still tend to employ only male workers.

**Early Marriage:** Early marriage of girls is a very common phenomenon in Bangladesh. Early marriage is one of the vital barriers to women's and girls' education, health and employment. Early marriage often results in early pregnancy which further reduces their employment opportunities as well as creating a vulnerable situation to their health.

Gender of the head of the family significantly influences the household decisions on an employment or not at all and also on the type of work that is done by women. This can be through individual or combination of adaptation strategies. As female-headed households often tend to suffer from labour shortages in Bangladesh, they are more likely to engage in informal sector activities.

Social restrictions on mobility and the burden of household responsibilities, in addition to cultural and social norms, also prevent women from participating in public life and employment for their livelihoods. In these cases, differences in the adoption of climate adaptation strategies can also result from inequities in endowments among male and female-headed households. Large endowment difference between men and women in terms of education is possible in Bangladesh because women are mostly involved in unpaid farm labour works.

5. Gender and the Women’s Development Policy (WDP)
In the context of the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Beijing Platform of Action, Bangladesh has developed several policies and sectoral strategies to ensure gender equality, including the Women’s Development Policy (WDP), 2011 and the National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan (NBSAP). The WDP is a comprehensive policy document that outlines specific measures to enhance the overall safety and security of women and children, including helping them deal with climate change. It also includes provisions for ensuring access to education and employment opportunities for disabled women and ensuring food distribution and assistance to eliminate bottlenecks created due to extreme climatic events and disasters. The proposed project will consider the following policies, strategies, and action plans.

Bangladesh has several policies and strategies to promote gender equality in agricultural development addressing climate change. The government recognizes the importance of having both women and men equally involved in decision-making processes related to agriculture and environmental management. However, despite affirmation from the government of its intention to mainstream gender in national and climate change strategies, such efforts remain inconsistently applied.

Key national laws and policies

4) National Plan on Disaster Management (NPM), 2016-2020 MoES & MoH

• DRM and emergency management are integrated in the disaster management policies.

5) National Sustainable Development Strategy

Focuses on the constitutional obligations of Bangladesh to have a people-centric approach with a vision for sustainable development.

6) Perspective Plan, 2017-2041 General Economics Division, Planning Commission

Considers both gender and environment as important perspectives for development by addressing those in separate chapters.

7) Mujib Climate Prosperity Plan (MCPP), Decade 2030

Intends to facilitate climate financing for vulnerable communities and to encourage women's empowerment. MCPP is formulated in honor of the Father of the Nation on his birth century.

8) National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA), 2009 MoEFCC

Suggests specific strategies for adaptation and recommends 15 projects, including gender-sensitive ones. NAPA was an urgent action exercise to address the current and anticipated adverse effects of climate change, including extreme events. NAPA was the first attempt to guide policy makers in formulating a national strategy for adapting to climate change at country level.

9) Climate Change and Gender Action Plan (CC&GAP), 2017 MoEFCC

Prepared with an aim to ensure the integration of gender equality into climate change-related policies, strategies and interventions. The eight pillars of the CC&GAP are: (i) food security; social protection and health; (ii) comprehensive disaster management; (iii) infrastructure; and (iv) institutional capacity building. It is in the process of being updated in light of the revised BCCSAP.

10) Eighth Five-Year Plan, 2020-2025

Acknowledges the role of women in the food and nutritional security of Bangladesh and takes steps to address barriers to productive participation of women in agricultural employment by addressing the following issues, amongst others:

• Social norms, preferences, and constraints that women endure in a male dominated society
• Wage differences between male and female in agriculture
• Women's access to institutions and facilities including

**Page 154**

After analyzing agriculture policies, it is evident that there are relevant policies in place that acknowledge that women are crucial for food processing and nutrition security. While women are missing from other roles in agriculture sector — as producers and other parts of the value chain — some of these policies do provide opportunities for women to participate in activities such as post-harvest activities. These policies suggest capacity-building measures, increased access to information, knowledge, financial assets, technology, farming resources and markets. These include microfinance schemes, training programs, extension services, and other support mechanisms to ensure women have equal opportunity in securing agricultural inputs like fertilizer, seed, farmer’s card and credit. However, these policies fall short in three aspects. First, the agriculture policies and strategies lack the analysis of differential impacts on men and women. Second, they do not consider the intersectionality of gender with particularly climate-gender interlinked measures. Third, lack of gender-disaggregated data on access to land, finance, extension services and agricultural tools act as a barrier to better gender-responsive climate action.

Mainstreaming of gender would continue and all macro-economic and sectoral policies would integrate gender equality in their design and implementation. This would reduce gender inequality and promote equal relationship between sexes. To ensure results from actions related to gender equality all reporting of national progress including those related to SDG 5 would be based on sex-disaggregated data. The framework for women’s empowerment and gender equality comprises three main pillars:

Improve women's human capabilities: This deals with women’s and girls’ access to health care, life expectancy, nutrition, reproductive health, education, information, training, and other services that enables them to achieve good health and educational outcomes. This also includes women’s freedom from violence and coercion.

Increase women’s economic benefits: This relates to women's access to land, inputs for their own productive assets, microfinance schemes, extension services, information technology, financial services, and other economic opportunities including community resources like water, land, forest etc.

Enhance women’s voice and agency: This pertains to women’s role as decision makers in public and private spheres. It also includes women’s ability to influence policy decisions and their changed attitudes on women's and girls' rights, women's enhanced knowledge of their rights and increasing their bargaining power within the family.

Create an enabling environment for women's advancement: The socio-political environment, legal and policy support, and congenial social norms are the key in this area. Oversight, enrollment of laws, regulations, policies and programs that promote gender equality is essential to build the capacity to develop, implement, and monitor gender strategies; understanding of gender issues in the sector are the key areas.

To implement these strategic objectives, seven action areas have been identified that will contribute in achieving results in these four areas:

i. Increase access to human development opportunities

ii. Enhance access and control over productive resources

iii. Increase women’s voice and agency

iv. Establish conducive legal and regulatory environment

v. Promote institutional capacity, accountability and oversight

vi. Increase protection and resilience from crisis and shocks

vii. Promote positive social norms

# Gender Mainstreaming in the Context of the Proposed Project

Gender mainstreaming is an integral part of the five-year project design. Hence, the project has analyzed gender-based vulnerabilities to climate change, particularly for women and girls who are affected more severely than men. The project also tried to identify gender-based violence and SEAH related risks, particularly due to implementation of the project activities.

## 6.1 Gender Mainstreaming in the Proposed Project Area

The impacts of climate change are gender-specific. A wide range of climate-change fallout (food insecurity, disrupted production, out-migration) are experienced differently by men and women. Women and girls who are already vulnerable due to different types of stressors such as natural disaster, economic and political factors are disproportionately affected by climate-related disasters. Furthermore, their vulnerability is a result of socio-economic and political factors that are exacerbated by climate change.

Women in coastal areas are exposed to many different types of stressors, both as natural disaster, particularly in the coastal areas. In this regard, Zhan (2022) conducted a study in Sabikha in the southwest part of Bangladesh using semi-structured and pre-tested interview schedule in order to assess the challenges of women during a cyclone event. A total of 60 households from Sabikha were randomly selected for better understanding of crab farming. It is evident from the study that, more than half (54%) of the respondents are involved in crab farming as their main livelihood. The majority of the respondents do not seek cyclone shelter due to a variety of reasons. In this regard, a major part of the respondents were not aware about the cyclone shelters and also they did not have access to the cyclone shelter. A large number of respondents stated that a major challenge in the shelter center is insufficient space for women and children. The study further revealed that 20% of the respondents suffered from diarrhea and nearly one-third from skin-related diseases. It is a matter of concern that a major part of the respondents suffers a lack of pure drinking water for their necessary use. Most of them were not aware about the cyclone shelters, which might have influenced their decision at the time of emergency due to socio-cultural trends.

Feridunabad (2019) conducted a study in Khulna, Bhogra and Sabikha in the southwest part of Bangladesh by using semi-structured and pre-tested interview schedule in order to assess the role of women in crab meat farming. A total of 150 households from Khulna; Bhogra; and Sabikha were randomly selected for better understanding of crab farming. About 74% women found to be involved in crab farming. A greater involvement found in Khulna area (78%). However, the study revealed that only a small number of women are involved in crab farming among the women in those three study areas. All of those findings revealed that anyone could practice the farming regardless of their age and education level. The study further revealed that there is no proper training development and women involvement in this sector has not been so long existence. A number of innovative technical supports are needed to disseminate the proper technologies to the women. In the present scenario, only 10% of the respondents were involved in crab farming as their main livelihood while 89.52% were involved in preparing pond before stocking. However, a small proportion (37%) of the respondents were involved in feeding and harvesting activities. This is due to some social religious norms and due to the pressure of other household works. Level of formal education among women was found to be low as compared with men. The study revealed that educated and more experienced mud crab farming farmers have found to more benefit that is in line with others finding established the positive impact of literacy over farm efficiency (Wang et al., 1996). From the comparison between the two groups, it can be seen that there was a significant difference (p<0.05) of significance) the net revenue earned from the crab farming indicating that higher involvement received more income than those who were not involved in crab farming. The study further revealed that the women get more and more training through different organizations could increase the revenue. During the period 2016-2019, there was a significant increase of female participation among the educated women in crab farming. In the recent year, there is rising awareness among the women through their participation in different income generating activities like aquaculture and other farming. From the study, lack of proper knowledge of crab farming and lack of training are addressed by women as


---
**Page 156**
---

problems for sustainable crab farming. Moreover, they also reported that their movement sometimes restricted by some religious norms.

The field visit report revealed significant gender-related vulnerabilities of coastal areas in terms of unequal wages, unsafe working conditions, violence, and access to food, limited education and inequalities in land ownership. Women are more vulnerable due to unequal access to employment opportunities is widespread discrimination in wages for paid work.

Due to the increasing economic crisis combined with sickness loss of the main breadwinner, many families have been forced into debt bondage. The increased vulnerability of women in village women are encountered economic insecurity, which is additionally increasing violence against women. In addition to this, women are also more vulnerable due to their lack of access to education and health services to preserve social order and secure the privilege under the social framework of patriarchy by maintaining their status as a subordinate gender.

Coastal disaster effects cause damage and loss to food production and resultant food price increases, creating economic insecurity for many families. The 2013 cyclone in Bangladesh is an example where this cyclone causes an extreme level of food crisis due to failures of crop production, vegetation loss, and declining wild fish. They sometimes eat only once daily because of these extreme climates. The only available food is rice or wheat which is not a nutritious diet for children. In addition, the water becomes salty, or waterless or jive or potable.

Coastal disaster effects limit failures of effective adaptation programs are responsible for many of these problems and also impact other rights such as access to education. Due to climate change vulnerabilities, women are unable to receive formal education and could not read and write. Low levels of education among women in coastal areas are a major cause of child marriage. The government informed us: "Early marriage requires lesser dowry, reducing the scope for girl education. Although the government has taken some steps towards providing free primary education and secondary education, they cannot afford the education for the next level like secondary or higher. Eve teasing on the way to school is a serious problem causes further challenges, and an old wife is less docile and more likely to be beaten up by her husband.

It is widely documented climate impacts on food production and access also disproportionately affect women in coastal areas. The 2013 cyclone in Bangladesh is another example that shows the strenuous economic conditions created by climate change are leading to an increase in child and bride marriages in Bangladesh, as dowries become cheaper". Compelling evidence from this research indicates that the government's current adaptation programs for coastal communities designed to ease both food insecurity and future financial pressures on families exacerbated as a result of climate change. The government has not taken any steps towards providing free higher education. There is also a much broader focus on social consequences in order to protect the human rights of women and girls in vulnerable communities.

could not leave without prior preparations, others died because their traditional clothing being got trapped in trees and other objects while running, and others perished trying to rescue or search for women who could not evacuate fast enough**. Furthermore, the cyclone was announced primarily among men and boys, and women were left behind to face the disaster alone in some areas and facing serious risks***. Disaster preparedness requires decision-making and leadership, but in coastal Bangladesh, women are generally excluded from such roles****. Post disaster stages also take a toll on women as they are often responsible for rebuilding their homes and communities with few alternatives, are exposed to urinary tract diseases, maybe sexually abused while looking for firewood or food, and may be forced into prostitution. Women who have been displaced are also offered more food to other household members and they lose the natural resources and livelihood assets that they had previously owned. The women in coastal Bangladesh are often described as having mentioned having been included in village disaster management committees and have been provided training and necessary equipment, such as early warning flags.

The above-mentioned facts highlight the gender-based discrimination and marginalized position in Bangladesh. For the analytical purpose, already mentioned that climate change itself does not directly affect the women, but rather it is the social, economic, and political factors that are responsible for the easily constructed system have made the situation possible where climate change plays a key role in mitigating the vulnerabilities. WHO notes, women and children are particularly affected by disasters, accounting for 80% of all disaster deaths. The vulnerability of women to natural disasters is due to effects of natural disaster and lack of health care, women are vulnerable to reproductive and sexual health problems, and they are more likely to be displaced from their homes. In the aftermath of a disaster that women become the primary caretakers for those affected by disasters-including children, the injured and sick. The loss of male family members in the household increases the vulnerability of women's vulnerability is further increased by the loss of men and/or livelihoods, especially when a male head of household has died and the woman must provide for their families. Post disaster stress symptoms are often but not universally reported more frequently by women than men***(Dingape et al., 2015, p.9).

Figure 1: Consolidated perspective of gender differentiated vulnerabilities linked to coastal areas of Bangladesh

Both during disasters and in the face of changing environmental conditions, women's role in communities is not formally recognized by national governments in mitigation, adaptation and relief efforts.

Women’s knowledge about ecosystems and their particular strategies, experiences and skills for coping with climate change are often ignored. The coastal zone of Bangladesh has 10,528 km² of land and 6,000 surface water ponds with saline water, used primarily by women for small vegetable farming and domestic livestock rearing. Women's vulnerability to climate change is directly related to the rehabilitation of these ponds.* Overall, women’s and girls’ vulnerability to climate change generally depends on their access to resources (AR), social capital (SC) and adaptive capacity (AC). The exposure is largely determined by the climatic hazards and the extent the women and girls are able to adapt. This paper aims at providing a consolidated perspective of the summary of the vulnerabilities of women and girls in the context of climate change in coastal areas in Bangladesh.

### 6.2 Assessment of SEAH Related Risks

There is less possibility to occur SEAH-related risks by the project interventions. Because selected women will be involved in many of the activities as these will be implemented at their homestead and neighbouring areas. In addition, some women may require to go away to work in the craft town or other places for a short period of time. The risk is that they might be exposed to sexual harassment and vice versa. However, the risk is very limited or negligible. But the challenge is that if any woman is affected by SEAH during her travel, she will not have access to any support at the project level; there may be female staff who will have to travel frequently in the selected villages. These staff may also be exposed to sexual harassment and vice versa.

These female staff would require frequent field visits in the remote areas of the country. Thus, they might be exposed to SEAH risks.

Different types of stakeholders will be involved during the implementation of the project. At the central level, PWOs, CCAOs, and other officials will be involved. At the district level, female staff are expected to be recruited. These staff will be required to travel in the remote areas alone or with male colleagues. In this case, the female staff may be exposed to SEAH risks. On the other hand, local women who are involved as volunteers for community mobilization, CCAG activities, monitoring physical interventions etc. They will also be required to travel in the remote areas and they might be exposed to sexual harassment and vice versa in various ways that include but are not limited to lack of sanitation facilities at work place, eve teasing, sexual exploitation and harassment; wage discrimination etc. An action plan matrix is developed to reduce the risk.

### 6.3 Action plan matrix for protection of GVB and SEAH

PKSF strictly follow government's policy on Sexual Harassment Free Educational and Working Environment (SEAWEE) Act, 2014. The following mitigation measures are taken to investigate and remedial measure against complaints on sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment.

| SLN | Identified risks | Mitigation measures | Responsibility | Source of Budget |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. | Wage discrimination | Awareness raising through CCAG meetings. | IE and CCAG members | No additional budget is required |
|  |  | Ensure equal payment to male and female labor during earth work. |  |  |
|  |  | Establish grievance redress mechanism at union level (the lowest administrative unit of Bangladesh). |  |  |
| 2. | Sexual harassment and/or eve teasing due to lack of sanitation facilities at work place | Establish separate sanitation facilities at the work place for both male and female staff. | IE and local contractors | Budget is built in the relevant activity. |
| 3. | Sexual harassment and/or eve teasing on the way to and from work place | Establish grievance redress mechanism under the supervision of the lowest administrative unit (the lowest administrative unit of Bangladesh). | IE under the supervision of |  |

7. Gender Mainstreaming in the Project

The project is well aware about gender mainstreaming in the project activities. Hence, the proposed project has taken a gender responsive and transformative approach to climate change vulnerability, considering gender equality and women empowerment as an integral part of its ability to plan adaptation and institutional support and capacity building; and this has fundamentally shaped all of the activities and outputs of the project. The impact of the proposed activities is captured in Figure 20. The proposed project recognizes that women are disproportionately affected by climate change, especially when it comes to a changing climate and resource constraints. The project will select 362,475 direct beneficiaries for transition from food insecurity to food security, out of which approximately 81% (94,000) of the total direct beneficiaries will be women. It is already mentioned that the project will form 3,200 CCAGs for support and capacity building in the project area. Considering the gender composition of the proposed project, 80% of the CCAG members will be women beneficiaries (among the beneficiaries under PKSF, around 95% are women). The project will select mostly women because they usually lead their households and are responsible for food security and nutrition. Women will also learn through training and meetings. Thus, climate change concepts and practices would transmit to the next generation as well. This is a very important aspect of gender mainstreaming in this country. Besides, necessary female staffs will be ensured at the field level so that women members can easily access the project activities. To ensure the quality of the project implementation and integrity, the project proposes more water facilities to outreach maximum number of women. Allocated budget for female beneficiaries also very high which is estimated USD328.89 million.

Besides, CGAP indicators showed that women’s benefits both economically and socially due to engage them with CCAGs in drought prone areas; women usually are not willing to go too far away


---
**Page 163**
---

from their house to collect water. They prefer to collect water from adjacent areas of their house because they can use their time in productive works. Most important lesson was that they could talk about climate and disaster in their locality. They also felt empowered because they contributed to famine kitchens, which were established by the community for those who lost their livelihoods while collecting water. We expect the similar outcomes in the proposed project. The CCGP faced some challenges to engage the CCAI with the women members of the families. Initially, the women in the vulnerable communities were not aware about climate change and its impact on their livelihoods as this was new issue to them. However, motivation through disseminating proper information helped to overcome these challenges.

The RHL project will accommodate Gudi's policies and strategies on women's resilience and their critical role in preparedness and recovery from disasters and the necessity of shifting livelihoods towards climate resilient ones. The Gender Assessment provides a comprehensive overview of women's access to resources, their role in decision-making and the gendered aspects of local livelihoods, and provides the basis for, and lessons on which, the Gender Action Plan (which is reflective of the overall project design) will be based. The GAP will provide a framework within which the project interventions that women can easily implement to enhance their capacity and increase their resilience to climate change.

For promoting women's empowerment through the project interventions, we will consult not only with the women members of a family, but also with the male members and other guardians. This will help eliminate gender-based barriers and ensure that Gudi will build good rapport by disseminating appropriate information with the vulnerable community.

The project will not only empower the women, but also sensitize the men about the role and responsibility of women in their family and to through CCQA meeting and training during the project period. Also, it will help to improve the social status of women and increase their role for boosting family income, social status and sharing responsibilities. In this way men will be assisting women to come to the costs value chain rather present.

# IMPROVE GVB BY CLIMATE CHANGE SCENARIOS

## Figure 2: The impact of the proposed activities

Overall:
- Increase women's participation in decision-making (contingency planning for disaster and climate resilience adaptation)
- Identify gaps in existing policies, programs, and services to address gender equality
- Account for differing needs of women and other marginalized groups in building climate resilience.
- Identify and address barriers to women's empowerment and inclusion in disaster risk reduction, including development of action plans to close these gaps, developing resources and expertise for implementation, and strengthening the capacity of national and local governments, civil society, and institutions accountable for outcomes that promote gender equality.

Training:
- Include training on climate change adaptation and gender equality in existing trainings aimed at drawing attention to the implication of climate resilience adaptation and gender equality.
- Undertake community dialogue in relation to gender and social inclusion in climate resilience.


For Training:
- Provision of women trainers and women-exclusive training sessions, including flexible times, and providing opportunities for women to participate in decision-making processes
- Integration of men and community elders in community trainings that addresses women's participation and norms around appropriate work for women, as well as mobility outside the household.


---
**Page 165**
---

For Livelihoods:
- Analysis of the gendered division of labour (gender differentiated responsibilities, and needs)
- Enhance working conditions where women are responsible at the household and community levels
- For hatcheries: segregated sanitation, ensuring equal pay for equal work, and access to the infrastructure required for production
- For household and community level agriculture and aquaculture-based livelihoods: Consider Shaded ponds, shallow water depth, and proximity to households

For Ownership of Assets:
- Enhance women's control over productive assets (finance, production skills, livestock assets and technology)
- Monitoring of productive assets and revenues, to ensure that revenues are kept in the hands of women or their beneficiaries.

For Market Integration:
- Access to finance, financial management training, and market prices for women
- Conservation of mangrove resources, wild stocks, and alternatives to mangrove fuel wood, given that women are often the collectors of wild crab fry and fuel wood from the mangrove areas.
- Give priority for employment in crab hatcheries.

For Grievances:
- Adoption of gender sensitive (and marginalized group sensitive) grievance mechanism, which allows for direct and unrestricted access, including provision of female GRM focal points

For Institutional Strengths:
- Improve institutional capacity, accountability and oversight (capacity of project executing and implementing agencies to ensure that the project is implemented in a gender-sensitive manner)
- Build on the projects, structures and initiatives being rolled out by the GoB and GCF in order to maximize the use of resources, and for greater efficiency and effectiveness.

8. Proposed Gender Action Plan:
The project's 4-year Gender Action Plan is to operationalize the constraints and opportunities for women and men that were identified during the gender analysis, towards fully integrating them into the project design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation phases. To ensure that this happens in a timely manner, particularly considering the disadvantaged position of women, steps will be taken to ensure that the results of the gender analysis are integrated into all aspects of the project design, implementation, and monitoring phases.
The project has a plan to undertake a "detailed gender gap analysis and action plan at the activity level" which is expected to be completed by June 2018. The Gender Action Plan will track progress on these actions at the activity level, which can be incorporated into the detailed M&E plan when it is developed in July 2018. The Gender Action Plan will also provide guidance and direction on how to ensure that the degree of gender-responsiveness and transformation continues to be measured and monitored throughout the project implementation period. It is expected that a separate document specifically for a Bangladesh context. Based on the approach a Gender logframe is developed for the project which is given in the Table-3 below.
